                                 Preface

TC 3-22 provides a training blueprint for all US Army forces who deploy to conduct Security
Force Assistance.

The principal audience for TC 3-22 is non-SFAB units who are tasked to conduct SFA.
However, units and personnel who are within an SFAB will also find this TC informative.
Trainers and educators throughout the Army will also use this publication.

Commanders, staffs, and subordinates ensure that their decisions and actions comply with
applicable United States, international, and in some cases host-nation laws and regulations.
Commanders at all levels ensure that their Soldiers operate in accordance with the law of war
and the rules of engagement. (See FM 6-27.)

TC 3-22 uses joint terms where applicable. Selected joint and Army terms and definitions
appear in both the glossary and the text. For other definitions shown in the text, the term is
italicized, and the number of the proponent publication follows the definition.

TC 3-22 applies to the Active Army, Army National Guard/Army National Guard of the United
States and United States Army Reserve unless otherwise stated.

The proponent of TC 3-22 is the United States Army Combined Arms Center, Mission
Command Center of Excellence. The preparing agency is the Combined Arms Doctrine
Directorate, United States Army Combined Arms Center. Send comments and
recommendations on DA Form 2028 (Recommended Changes to Publications and Blank
Forms) to Commander, United States Army Combined Arms Center, and Fort Leavenworth,
ATZL-MCD (TC,3-22), 300 McPherson Avenue, Fort Leavenworth, KS 66027-2337; by email
to usarmy.leavenworth.mccoe.mbx.cadd-org-mailbox@army.mil; or submit an electronic DA
Form 2028.

                                                                                                                               Introduction

                              Introduction

Security Force Assistance is one of the most important things that our Army does. This is a
mission that is executed across the continuum of conflict. US Army forces work with our
partners and allies during competition to set conditions to prevail in combat by fighting from a
position of advantage or dissuade our enemies from initiating a conflict. In crisis, US advisors
are able to rapidly embed in partner nation formations and enable those partners to significantly
increase their combat power by supporting them with US enablers and intelligence. This rapid
increase in coalition combat power can have a significant deterrent effect in a crisis situation.
Last but not least, if combat operations are required, US forces can again embed with partner
nation forces and significantly increase their combat power through effective advice and US
enabler support.

This Training Circular provides guidance to US forces on how to plan, prepare, and execute a
Security Force Assistance mission. The US Army has specialized forces, the Security Force
Assistance Brigades, who focus on this mission. However, demand for SFA forces and
advisors greatly exceeds capacity. Therefore, non-SFAB forces are frequently called upon to
deploy and conduct Security Force Assistance missions. The manual is intended for the entire
force, but it is particularly focused on those forces outside of SFABs who are frequently called
upon to deploy and conduct Security Force Assistance. The TC is written primarily through
the lens of deployment during competition, but throughout the TC one thread remains strong:
all SFA forces deployed in competition must always be prepared for a change of mission. Our
adversaries may choose to create a crisis or conflict at any time. In that case, those forces
deployed to conduct SFA during competition may rapidly find themselves in the midst of a
crisis or conflict. By following the guidance in this Training Circular, US Army forces will be
prepared to execute SFA missions across the continuum of conflict.

Training Circular 3-22 uses the Operations Process of Plan, Prepare, Execute, and Assess to
form the basis for the Training Circular.

Chapter 1 provides an overview of concepts and terms that are relevant to Security Force
Assistance

Chapter 2 focuses on the pre-deployment tasks of Plan and Prepare. It provides a detailed
timeline of pre-deployment tasks and a schedule to execute them.

Chapter 3 is focused on Execution. This includes details on RIP/TOA if a unit is relieving
another unit and actions taken if arriving as an initial unit as part of an episodic SFA mission,
or the lead element of a recurring mission. In particular, this chapter focuses on preparedness
to immediately transition to conflict or crisis if required.

Chapter 4 focuses on the final step of the Operations Process, Assess. It also includes a detailed
timeline of redeployment tasks, starting with tasks that must be conducted while in theater and
going through tasks conducted at home station after redeployment. It must be noted that
Assessments happen before, during, and after a deployment. Thus, although the assessment
chapter is the last chapter since it is the final step in the Operations Process, several portions of
this chapter apply to pre-deployment and execution activities.

Multiple appendixes provide greater detail on specific topics of interest. These are referred to throughout
the main body chapters as appropriate.

                                                 Chapter 1

                        Concepts and Definitions

    This chapter initiates with a detailed definition of security cooperation. It then progresses to
    explore the objectives underlying security cooperation, delineating the various programs and
    activities that constitute this domain, as well as elucidating the significant role played by the
    Army in the realm of security cooperation. Subsequently, the focus shifts to an in-depth
    examination of the planning processes and organizational structures pertinent to security
    cooperation. The chapter culminates with an insightful analysis of operational and strategic
    approaches to mitigating risks associated with security cooperation.

SECTION I – STRATEGIC CONTEXT

     1-1. The strategic context for security cooperation is multifaceted and dynamic, driven by a combination of
     global security challenges, national interests, international laws, alliances, and a range of economic, political,
     and technological factors.

SECURITY COOPERATION DEFINED

     1-2. Security cooperation is defined as Department of Defense interactions with foreign security
     establishments to build relationships that promote specific United States security interests, develop allied and
     partner military and security capabilities for self-defense and multinational operations, and provide United
     States forces with peacetime and contingency access to allies and partners. (JP 3-20).

THE PURPOSE OF SECURITY COOPERATION

     1-3. The purpose behind security cooperation (SC) is to enable the joint force to leverage the capabilities of
     our allies and partners to meet strategic objectives by building combat power and increasing available
     maneuver space. Strategic competition highlights the requirement for combatant commands to shape an
     operational environment (OE) to deter adversaries and enable U.S. forces and our allies and partners to fight
     from a position of advantage in conflict. By conducting SC, the Army can add additional capability and create
     options for the joint force commander to engage, deter, and defeat adversaries through the most efficient use
     of U.S. resources.

     1-4. Although the Department of State (DOS) leads the whole-of-government approach and provides
     oversight to SC, most Army activities are carried out and coordinated by, with, or through the theater army
     throughout the combatant command area of responsibility (AOR). These activities include—

          • Develop and apply capabilities and capacity.
          • Provide U.S. forces with access.
          • Build relationships that promote specific U.S. security interests.

     1-5. Security cooperation missions should be tied directly to supporting theater army campaign plans and
     potential contingency plans. SC activities are designed to develop partner capability, increase capacity, and
     enable interoperability. Additionally, SC missions can be used to enable access to specific key terrain needed
     across domains to provide maneuver space for possible contingency plans.

     1-6. SC is comprised of three different activities:
          • Security Force Assistance (SFA).
          • Security Assistance (SA)
          • Military engagement.

SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE

     1-7. Security Force Assistance is Department of Defense (DOD) activities that support the development of
     the d capacity of foreign security forces and their supporting institutions. (JP 3-20)

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  1


---

Chapter 1

     1-8. Consistent with DOD policy for SFA, the Army develops, maintains, and institutionalizes the
     capabilities of its personnel to support DOD efforts to organize, train, equip, build, rebuild, advise, support,
     and liaise with FSF and relevant supporting institutions.

     1-9. Security forces are duly constituted military, paramilitary, police, and constabulary forces of a state (JP
     3-22). When directed to do so in accordance with appropriate legal authorities, Army forces conduct security
     force assistance activities in support of combatant command campaign plans and national objectives.

SECURITY ASSISTANCE

     1-10. SA is a group of programs authorized by the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, as amended, and the
     Arms Export Control Act of 1976, as amended, or other related statutes by which the United States provides
     defense articles, military training, and other defense-related services by grant, loan, credit, or cash sales in
     furtherance of national policies and objectives. (JP 3-20).

     1-11. SA programs are an element of security cooperation funded under Title 22, United States Code (22
     USC) and managed by the DOS. The DOD is the executive agent for SA under the DOS and the Defense
     Security Cooperation Agency (DSCA) administers the program for the DOD. SA programs are typically
     focused on the transfer of defense articles and services to eligible foreign governments, the provision of
     training and education to FSF, and the sale of construction services in support of partner nations’ military
     establishments. (FM 3-22).

MILITARY ENGAGEMENT

     1-12. Military engagement is contact and interaction between individuals or elements of the Armed Forces
     of the United States and those of another nation’s armed forces, or foreign and domestic civilian authorities
     or agencies, to build trust and confidence, share information, coordinate mutual activities, and maintain
     influence (JP 3-0).

     1-13. Military engagement is one of the primary ways SC practitioners achieve influence. These key leader
     engagements occur at all echelons up to the highest levels of the Army staff and sometimes include major
     combined staff talks. Although it is common practice for military personnel to engage their counterparts in
     other militaries at all echelons, it is still critical for individuals and organizations conducting military
     engagement to understand and remember that the DOS has primary oversight for the diplomatic instrument
     of national power (FM 3-22).

THEATER COMMAND

     1-14. Theater Commands are the primary managers of Security Cooperation activities. They serve as the
     regional arms that plan, coordinate, and execute initiatives aimed at strengthening alliances, building partner
     capacity, and promoting regional stability.

COMBATANT COMMAND

     1-15. In accordance with the Unified Command Plan, combatant commands are established by the President,
     through the Secretary of Defense, with the advice and assistance of the CJCS. Combatant commands are
     assigned AORs and work in support of, and in close coordination with U.S. embassies within their AORs to
     achieve U.S. national interests and global end states as detailed in the NSS and other guidance documents.
     This operational relationship is primarily related to the SC organization’s execution of the combatant
     command’s theater campaign plan with the host country’s military. The combatant command and the
     ambassador ensure that the SDO/DATT does not receive conflicting guidance, instructions, or priorities. If
     conflicting guidance occurs, the SDO/DATT must seek clarification or resolution. The SDO/DATT chief is
     uniquely able to understand both the combatant command and the ambassador, balance their respective
     priorities, and leverage their resources. A key challenge for the SDO/DATT is to respond to the direction of
     the ambassador while at the same time satisfying coordination and support requirements from the combatant
     command.

     1-16. DOD’s strategic end states support the NSS to ensure the strategic end states complement and support
     foreign policy objectives. As part of theater campaign planning, combatant commands develop country plans
     that align with each ambassador’s goals within the AOR because the activities and investments typically
     occur at the country level. Combatant commands exercise authority for force protection over all DOD

2          TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                                  Concepts and Definitions

     personnel (including their dependents) assigned, attached, transiting through, or training in the combatant
     command’s AOR, except for those for whom the COM retains security responsibility. This force protection
     authority enables combatant commands to change, modify, prescribe, and enforce force protection measures
     for covered forces. Combatant commands also provide directive authority over forces conducting exercises
     in that AOR.

THEATER ARMY ROLE

     1-17. The Army normally executes its 10 USC, responsibilities to organize, train, and equip operational
     Army units through the theater army to support combatant command objectives. The theater army sets and
     maintains the theater for the conduct of multidomain operations. Setting the theater includes posturing Army
     forces and conducting SC activities that shape an OE and prevent conflict. Setting the theater shapes the
     environment and produces the conditions necessary for the joint force commander, when directed, to seize,
     retain, and exploit the initiative and assure freedom of action. Sustained military engagement requires a
     combination of integrated regionally aligned conventional forces and ARSOF. Military engagement, along
     with intelligence collection and assured access, is critical to enabling the force to win decisively. Setting the
     theater includes theater opening; port and terminal operations; conducting reception, staging onward
     movement, and integration; force modernization and theater-specific training; Army support to other
     Services; as well as common user logistics to Army, joint, and multinational forces operating in the theater.

     1-18. The theater army develops a theater campaign support plan in an annex to the combatant command
     campaign plan. The theater campaign support plan serves as the mechanism between planning, programming,
     budgeting, and execution processes by, with, or through the theater army. It is supported by Headquarters,
     Department of the Army; functional Army Service component commands (ASCCs); Army commands; direct
     reporting units (known as DRUs); and the Reserve Component to resource SC activities that shape an OE
     and achieve combatant command campaign plan (CCP) objectives and joint strategic campaign plan end
     states.

     1-19. The theater army is the ASCC to the combatant command and fulfills all requirements of a Service
     component for Army forces assigned or attached to the combatant command. The theater army’s primary
     responsibilities are—

          • Execute combatant commander’s daily operational requirements.
          • Provide administrative control of Army forces.
          • Set and maintain the theater.
          • Set and support the operational areas.
          • Plan and coordinate the consolidation of gains in support of joint operations.
          • Exercise command and control over Army forces in theater.
          • Perform joint roles in limited scope, scale, and duration.

      1-20. ATP 3-93 depicts the organization of the theater army main command post. The theater army SC
      division normally resides within the assistant chief of staff G-3. It normally consists of a headquarters
      section, a plans and exercises section, and an international military affairs section with the total division
      consisting of eight to ten personnel and normally led by a colonel or lieutenant colonel.

SECTION II — U.S. EMBASSIES

      1-21. The US Embassy serves as the primary representative of the US Government in a foreign country.
      The US Ambassador, or Chief of Mission, is the direct representative of the US President. Each ambassador
      reports directly to the President. Within the embassy are representatives from multiple US agencies,
      including the Department of Defense. The number of agencies represented will depend on the size of the
      embassy. Each of these agency representatives reports to the US Ambassador, who has authority and
      responsibility for all US government activities in the foreign nation. See Appendix I, US Embassy, for
      detailed information on US Embassies.

      1-22. Department of Defense representation at U.S. Embassies overseas is generally divided into two
      principal offices with separate but mutually supportive roles, responsibilities, and authorities. These are the
      defense attaché office and the security cooperation organization. The defense attaché is also typically the
      senior defense official in country and referred to by the title SDO/DATT (Figure 1).

---

Chapter 1

   Figure 1-1. U.S. Government organization for security assistance and security cooperation

Senior Defense Official/Defense Attaché

      1-23. The Senior Defense Official/Defense Attaché (SDO/DATT) is the Chief of Mission’s (CoM)
      principal diplomatically accredited military advisor and representative of all Department of Defense (DOD)
      elements within the country team, except for the Marine Security Guard detachment. The SDO/DATT is
      the single point of contact for all DOD elements working within or from the embassy.

Defense Attaché Office

      1-24. The defense attaché office is an office of Service attachés managed by the Defense Intelligence
      Agency. The senior defense official/ defense attaché heads the defense attaché office in country and is a
      member of the country team. The senior defense official/defense attaché is the principal military advisor to
      the chief of mission on defense and national security issues, the senior diplomatically accredited Department
      of Defense military officer assigned to a U.S. diplomatic mission, and the single point of contact for all
      Department of Defense matters involving the embassy or Department of Defense elements assigned to or
      working from the embassy. The defense attaché is normally the senior Service attaché assigned to the

4          TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                               Concepts and Definitions

mission. The senior defense official/defense attaché is the in-country focal point for planning, coordinating,
and executing support to U.S. Government officials for in-country U.S. defense issues and activities that are
not under the purview of the parent Department of Defense components. The attachés serve as liaisons with
their host-nation counterparts and are valuable sources of information for the chief of mission and
geographic combatant commander on the military affairs of the host nation. The defense attaché may be
accredited to more than one country. The senior defense official/defense attaché is also the in-country
representative of the Secretary of Defense, the Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, and the geographic
combatant commander, and is responsible (under the direction of the chief of mission) for coordinating
administrative and security matters for all Department of Defense elements assigned to the country (except
those under the control of a geographic combatant commander). The attachés assist in the foreign internal
defense program by exchanging information with the geographic combatant commander’s staff on host-
nation military, political, humanitarian, religious, social, and economic conditions, and interagency
coordination.

Security Cooperation Organization

1-25. The mission of the security cooperation organization is to enhance the long-term bilateral defense
relationship between the host-country and the United States. This is accomplished by managing the many
programs that fall under the security assistance and security cooperation umbrella. These include
International Military Education and Training (IMET), Foreign Military Sales (FMS), Foreign Military
Financing (FMF), and special foreign assistance authorities such as Global Train and Equip (initially
authorized by Section 1206 of the National Defense Authorization Act for Fiscal Year 2006). The security
cooperation organization also manages humanitarian and civic assistance projects and serves as the principal
U.S. Embassy liaison for the National Guard State Partnership Program and other training and assistance
programs. The security cooperation organization chief serves as the geographic combatant command
representative for cooperative security assistance to the country team and coordinates with the Defense
Security Cooperation Agency.

1-26. A security cooperation organization typically consists of one to three U.S. military officers and may
employ several foreign-service nationals. Not all Embassies have a specific security cooperation
organization; rather, some may have only had a defense attaché office or no military personnel at all. In many
countries, security cooperation and assistance functions are performed within the defense attaché office. The
structure and existence of these offices may differ depending on the size of the U.S. mission, the nature of
the bilateral relationship with the host-nation, or the organization of the geographic combatant command.

1-27. Security cooperation organization is a generic term that refers to Department of Defense organizations
in a foreign country to carry out security cooperation and security assistance management tasks. These
organizations and offices will have their own formal titles or designations—some established through joint
diplomatic agreement between the United States and host nation.

Table 1-1 provides a list of the current security cooperation organization designations around the world. The
term security cooperation organization is used throughout this publication.

DEPARTMENT OF DEFENSE FORCE PROTECTION DETACHMENT

      1-28. A third Department of Defense office that may be encountered is a Department of Defense force
      protection detachment. The primary mission of force protection detachment is to detect and warn of threats
      to Department of Defense personnel (military, civilian, and dependents) and resources in-transit at overseas
      locations without a permanent Department of Defense counterintelligence presence. Force protection
      detachments serve as force protection multipliers for the U.S. Embassy country team in support of the
      Department of Defense presence in those overseas locations. Force protection detachments work directly
      with the regional security office to ensure the safety and security of Department of Defense personnel under
      the force-protection authority of the geographic combatant command.

SECTION III — SFA FORCES

      1-29. The Army conducts SFA through both specialized forces who have SFA as a core part of their mission
      essential task list, as well as general purpose forces who are not trained, organized and equipped to conduct
      SFA but may receive a task to conduct an SFA mission for a specified duration of time.

     1-30. Organizations who are organized, trained, and equipped to conduct SFA include Security Force
     Assistance Brigades (SFABs), Special Forces Groups and some other SOF forces, and Digital Liaison
     Detachments (DLDs).

     1-31. Army forces who regularly conduct SFA but are not organized, trained, and equipped to do so include
     National Guard forces operating under the SPP (State Partnership Program), regionally aligned forces
     assigned to combatant commands for general purpose missions but utilized to conduct SFA, and CONUS
     based forces who are specifically requested by the combatant command through an RFF to execute a specific
     SFA mission, often due to specific technical skill sets.

FORCES SPECIALLY TRAINED TO CONDUCT SFA

     1-32. The security force assistance brigade (SFAB) is the Army’s dedicated conventional organization for
     conducting SFA, and its capabilities allow it to execute SFA developmental activities with the partner FSF
     in conjunction with unified action partners anywhere in the world. The SFAB complements the DOS, foreign
     area officers, ARSOF, regionally aligned forces, SA teams, State Partnership Program, digital liaison
     detachments, and other unified action partner activities to expand access and create options for senior military
     and civilian leaders. During competition and campaigning, each combatant command is allocated a persistent
     SFAB force package. Conducting security cooperation activities under the threshold of armed conflict, the
     SFAB often falls under operational and administrative control of the theater army. The combatant commander
     assigns tactical- or operational-level SFA developmental activities to the SFAB. Advisor teams can establish

6                  TC 3-22                                           29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                                  Concepts and Definitions

     relationships among other conventional force partners to enable the rapid integration of other multinational
     forces that may be essential during future multidomain operations (ATP 3-96.1).

     1-33. Digital liaison detachments are assigned or attached to selected theater armies for employment at the
     theater army or in support at corps and division echelons. These teams provide SFA to the partner FSF
     through digital information management and communications interface capability for U.S. systems with a
     subordinate or parallel joint and multinational headquarters in an operational area. Digital liaison
     detachments are typically embedded in their counterpart staffs to facilitate mission command by clarifying
     orders, interpreting commander’s intent, and identifying and resolving issues (ATP 3-94.1).

     1-34. Army SOF are particularly well suited to conduct SFA due to their advanced skills, capabilities, and
     regional orientation. SFA and FID overlap without being subsets of each other (see figure 1-1 on page 1-3).
     At operational and strategic levels, both SFA and FID focus on developing partner FSF internal capacity and
     capability, however SFA also prepares the partner FSF to defend against external threats and to perform as
     part of an international coalition. FID and SFA are similar at the tactical level where advisory skills are
     applicable to both (FM 3-05).

SFA PRACTICIONERS WITHOUT SPECIALIZED TRAINING

     1-35. The State Partnership Program (SPP) is a joint DOD SC program authorized by 10 USC, Section 341,
     and governed by DODI 5111.20. The SPP is managed by the National Guard Bureau, executed by the
     combatant commands, and sourced by the National Guard of the states and territories. The SPP links a State’s
     National Guard with a partner nation’s military, security forces, and disaster response organizations in a
     cooperative, mutually beneficial relationship. SPP is one of the few SC programs authorized to engage across
     the spectrum of country governmental organizations and is a multi-categorization SC tool. Historically, the
     majority of SFA missions conducted in many theaters are conducted through the SPP

     1-36. Regionally aligned and allocated forces provide the combatant command with tailored, responsive, and
     consistently available Army forces. They meet combatant command requirements for scalable, tailored
     capabilities to support operational missions, military exercises, and theater SC activities. They represent those
     Army units assigned to combatant commands, allocated to a combatant command, and those capabilities
     distributed and prepared by the Army for combatant command regional missions. Assigned units have a
     regional mission and training focus, including emerging operations, security cooperation activities, and
     exercises.

SECTION IV — ADVISORS

      1-37. In the complex landscape of modern military operations, the role of an advisor is both critical and
      multifaceted. Advisors serve as a vital link between various stakeholders, including military units,
      governmental agencies, and sometimes even non-governmental organizations. They operate under different
      legal and operational frameworks, which define their scope of work and set the boundaries for their roles
      and responsibilities. This guide aims to provide a comprehensive overview of the roles an advisor may be
      called upon to perform, from observation and mentorship to mediation and legal compliance. It integrates
      various aspects of advisory roles, including the legal frameworks that govern them, the complex
      environments in which they operate, and the specific tasks they may be required to undertake. The guide
      serves as an essential resource for understanding the intricacies involved in being an effective advisor in a
      conventional military setting.

ADVISOR ROLES, SELECTION AND REQUIRED SKILLS AND TECHNIQUES

      1-38. The role of an advisor within a conventional military force is a complex and multi-dimensional one,
      often requiring a delicate balance of observation, mentorship, and mediation. These roles are not just titles
      but encompass a range of responsibilities and tasks that the advisor must skillfully navigate. Whether it's
      providing a ground-level operational picture to higher command, fostering a strong emotional bond with the
      unit for effective mentorship, or serving as a diplomatic mediator between different parties, an advisor's
      roles are critical for mission success. This section aims to delineate these roles in detail, offering a
      comprehensive understanding of what each entail and how they intersect and sometimes conflict. It also

Chapter 1

      integrates considerations for legal and operational frameworks, providing a holistic view of the advisor's
      function in a conventional military se

      1-39. tting.

      1-40. Screening and selection of advisors often proves to be the single most important step in executing an
      SFA mission. Appendix A gives detailed guidance on advisor screening and selection.

      1-41. Advising techniques. ATP 3-07.10 provides excellence Tactics, Techniques, and Procedures for
      advisors. This Multi-Service MTTP has been adopted by all service components within DOD as the single
      authoritative manual for advisors from the tactical to the strategic level. After selection, all advisors must
      familiarize themselves with this manual as part of their individual training path. Additionally, the MATA
      (Military Advisor Training Academy) at Ft. Moore, GA runs a 6-week advisor training course that focuses
      on training advisor skills. This course is open to all DOD members via ATRRS and awards the Advisor
      skill identifier.

                                                 Chapter 2

        Advisory Mission Pre-deployment Planning and
                                  Preparation

    This chapter focuses on the tasks that need to be accomplished prior to execution of an SFA
    mission. The chapter is written from the perspective of a unit being alerted to deploy for an
    SFA mission and having 9-10 months’ time to prepare. Many SFA practitioners may have less
    time to prepare for their mission, ranging from 24 weeks to 24 days to 24 hours. In situations
    where a shortened timeline is forced, leaders must make timely decisions on what tasks can
    and cannot be accomplished, then inform their higher HQ of the resulting risk.

    This chapter begins by discussing assessments in detail. This starts with an overview of the
    Assessment, Monitoring, and Evaluation process used in Security Cooperation and compares
    that to Army doctrine on assessments. Since this TC follows the Operations Process paradigm
    of plan, prepare, execute, assess, there is an entire chapter on assessments later in the TC.
    However, that chapter is dealing with post-operations assessments. This chapter begins by
    talking about the initial assessments. The chapter than goes on to talk about predeployment
    planning, and then gives a detailed timeline of predeployment tasks to plan and prepare for an
    SFA mission.

SECTION I OVERVIEW OF SECURITY COOPERATION ASSESSMENT,
    MONITORING, AND EVALUATION ACTIVITIES

    Assessment, Monitoring, and Evaluation are essential for ensuring that Security Cooperation
    activities are well-targeted, efficiently executed, and effective in achieving their goals. They
    provide the data and insights needed to make informed decisions, improve program design, and
    maximize the impact of security cooperation efforts.

PURPOSE AND FUNCTIONS OF ASSESSMENT, MONITORING, AND
EVALUATION (AM&E):

     2-1. In the complex world of Security Cooperation (SC) activities, the assessment, monitoring, and
     evaluation (AM&E) of Foreign Security Forces (FSF) plays a critical role. This process begins with initial
     assessments which are essential for establishing a successful advising engagement with a Partner Nation
     (PN). These initial assessments serve dual purposes: they inform the design of advising activities, such as a
     Combatant Commander's (CCDR) plan, and provide a baseline for accurately measuring the progress of SC
     activities and the enabling programs. Monitoring activities continue during the execution of the mission, and
     Evaluations provide critical input on the achievement of objectives.

Assessment, Monitoring, and Evaluation Activities (AM&E) in Joint and Army
Doctrine

     2-2. Department of Defense’s policy on AM&E for Security Cooperation is outlined in DODI 5132.14. This
     DODI is based on the Congressionally directed requirements for AM&E that are directed in each annual
     NDAA (National Defense Authorization Act). The NDAA is re-issued every year, so specific AM&E
     requirements frequently change. The term AM&E is not widely used elsewhere in DOD, but it is used for
     Security Cooperation and is used extensively by the DOS and other inter-agency partners when referring to
     Security Cooperation. Note that DOS uses the term “Security Sector Assistance” rather than Security
     Cooperation, but the two terms are nearly identical in meaning. (FM 3-22).

     2-3. Army doctrine lays out 3 types of assessments: pre-operation assessments, during operation
     assessments, and post-operation assessments. In common Army usage, the stand-alone term “assessment”

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  9


---

Chapter 2

     often refers to post-operations assessments. Multiple Army processes use the word “Assessment” in this
     manner. For example, the Operations Process that this TC uses as a format methodology for SFA missions
     is Plan, Prepare, Execute, Assess. Similarly, the Army’s Targeting Process of D3A is Decide, Detect, Deliver,
     Assess. We take the time to highlight this fact in TC 3-22 because the common Army usage of the term
     “Assess” differs from the usage of the word “Assess” when it is used in the AM&E framework for Security
     Cooperation. In the AM&E framework, assess generally equates to the pre-operations assessment in Army
     doctrine, while Monitor and Evaluate more closely equate to the during-operations and post-operations
     assessments respectively.

     2-4. Joint Publication 3-20 further expounds on DODI 5132.14 by adding Recommend and Direct to AM&E.
     This results in JP 3-20 calling for an Initial Assessment, followed by the detailed assessment activities of
     Monitor, Evaluate, Recommend and Direct. This addition of Recommend and Direct in JP 3-20 is not
     recognized by DODI 5132.14 nor is it used by DSCA or the interagency community. Most significantly,
     SFA practitioners need to be aware of the Security Cooperation usage of Assess, Monitor and Evaluate as
     used in DODI 5132.14 rather than the common Army usage of the term “Assess” to ensure they are utilizing
     a common language with our inter-agency partners when operating in the Security Cooperation /Security
     Sector Assistance realm.

ASSESSMENT ACTIVITIES

     2-5. Detailed assessment activities involve a comprehensive evaluation of the security environment, partner
     capabilities, needs, cultural and political context, risks, and stakeholder perspectives. These activities provide
     the foundation for effective planning, implementation, and evaluation of Security Cooperation efforts,
     ensuring that they are well-targeted, contextually appropriate, and aligned with strategic objectives.

ASSESSMENT (INITIAL)

     2-6. Key elements of these initial assessments focus on three fundamental and interrelated considerations.
     First is the level of progress towards building a robust security relationship with the PN, which is crucial for
     understanding the depth and efficacy of cooperation. Second is the assessment of the FSF's capability and
     capacity, offering insights into their operational readiness and potential areas for development. The third
     aspect is the assessment of U.S. access within the PN, including the anticipated freedom of action, which
     hinges on the bilateral relationship between the United States and the PN.

MONITORING PROCESS

     2-7. Monitoring in SC activities involves a comprehensive and ongoing observation of joint forces and FSF.
     Advisors, working closely with their counterparts, play a crucial role in this process. They focus on
     understanding how partner forces and mission accomplishment evolve over time, even across multiple
     advisor missions. Monitoring uses Measures of Performance (MOPs) and Measures of Effectiveness (MOEs)
     to track FSF tasks and informs decisions at strategic, operational, and tactical levels. This involves assessing
     whether tasks are completed as planned, within the stipulated timeline, and within projected costs.

     2-8. End-Use-Monitoring (EUM) is a specific monitoring requirement dictated by Congress. It is often
     associated with the SA activities of Foreign Military Sales and may consist of such things as serial number
     inventories and monitoring to determine if law of war violations have occurred. SFA practitioners will rarely
     be asked to directly participate in EUM, but they may assist the SCO in EUM while monitoring the progress
     of their partner forces.

EVALUATION PROCESS

     2-9. Evaluation, an essential aspect of the commander's role, can occur at any level. It involves the systematic
     collection and analysis of information about ongoing or completed SC activities. This process assesses an
     advisor mission's relevance, efficiency, sustainability, and impact, with a focus on documenting
     achievements, attaining end states, and determining the value of continuing advising activities. Evaluations
     are typically administered by teams from a Combatant Command (CCMD), Subordinate Unified Command
     (SCC), or Theater Special Operations Command (TSOC) headquarters, with advisors contributing objective
     assessments and data. These evaluations are critical at the strategic level and depend on operational
     evaluations to understand why certain efforts succeed or fail.

10  TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                 Advisory Mission Pre-deployment Planning and Preparation

SECTION II – DEPLOYMENT PLANNING

    Deployment planning is a critical and complex phase, faced with various challenges such as
    resource constraints, cultural differences, and logistical complexities. Collaborative planning
    across different levels of command is essential to ensure a well-prepared and effective advising
    mission. See ATP 3-07.10 for more information.

INITIAL COORDINATING AND PLANNING EVENTS

     2-10. Conduct an in-depth mission analysis to determine readiness and initiate necessary coordination for
     entering and operating within the host country. This phase sets the foundation for a successful deployment
     and operation.

MULT ECHELON PLANNING

     2-11. Establish clear command and support relationships. Assess and integrate operational requirements with
     the mission's objectives. Develop and refine measures of performance (MOPs) and measures of effectiveness
     (MOEs) for various courses of action, ensuring alignment with broader security objectives and plans.

FUNDING AND RESOURCE ALLOCATION

     2-12. Identifying appropriate funding sources and coordinating with related agencies for mission support is
     pivotal. This includes understanding the implications of different funding authorities and ensuring that
     financial resources are aligned with the mission's needs and objectives.

PRE-MISSION PLANNING AND CULTURAL ACCLIMATIZATION

     2-13. Thorough review and analysis of existing campaign and country plans are essential. This helps in
     identifying gaps in capabilities or capacities of the host nation's forces. Additionally, a deep understanding
     of the host nation's culture, politics, and social dynamics is crucial for developing rapport and effective
     collaboration.

HOST NATION APPROVAL AND COUNTRY CLEARANCES

     2-14. Navigating the visa application process and ensuring compliance with country clearance requirements
     are critical administrative tasks. Efficient handling of these processes prevents delays and ensures smooth
     entry and operation within the host country.

LEGAL BRIEFINGS AND COMPLIANCE

     2-15. Comprehensive briefings on the rules of engagement, local laws, and customs are vital. These briefings,
     typically provided by the Staff Judge Advocate, ensure that the advising team operates within the legal
     boundaries and respects the host nation's legal framework.

DETAILED PREDEPLOYMENT SITE SURVEYS

     2-16. Conducting thorough site surveys provides essential insights into the operational environment. These
     surveys inform the advising team about command structures, support mechanisms, and sustainment
     relationships, vital for mission success.

MEDICAL AND HEALTH CONSIDERATIONS

     2-17. Assessing medical capabilities in relation to the mission's requirements is crucial. This includes
     understanding local medical infrastructure, evacuation procedures, and availability of medical supplies,
     ensuring the health and safety of the team.

ADVISOR TEAM COMPOSITION AND TRAINING

     2-18. Tailoring the advising team to fit the specific requirements of the mission is essential. This involves
     assessing the proficiency levels and resources of the host nation's forces and organizing the advising team
     accordingly. Continuous mission analysis supports a range of advising tasks, from institutional instruction to
     tactical training.

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  11


---

Chapter 2

OPERATIONAL ENVIRONMENT AND RISK ASSESSMENT

     2-19. Understanding the degree of unit dispersion and preparing for independent operations if necessary is
     critical. This includes considerations for communication, supply chains, and emergency procedures, ensuring
     operational effectiveness and safety in a dispersed environment.

PERSONNEL RECOVERY AND SAFETY

     2-20. Developing and refining personnel recovery plans is essential, especially for geographically dispersed
     deployments. These plans should be regularly reviewed and updated to ensure the safety and security of the
     advising team members.

SECTION III — DEPLOYMENT–300 TO DEPLOYMENT

    Deployment is a carefully planned operation. The path to deployment is punctuated by a
    sequence of essential tasks crafted to help ensure the deploying unit is ready for its mission.
    From the Geographic Combatant Command issuing task orders to the collaboration with Army
    Service Component Commands and Security Cooperation Organizations, each step is vital.
    This document presents a detailed timeline of tasks that need to be completed prior to
    deployment.

D–300 TASKS

     2-21. The following sections details tasks broken down by a recommended NLT day to have the task
     completed. In many cases, units will be alerted for deployment inside of this timeline. In that case, unit
     commands may have to determine which tasks they will have to assume risk with and inform their higher
     headquarters of the risk taken.

GEOGRAPHIC COMBATANT COMMAND ISSUES TASK ORDER

                Network. The deploying element higher headquarters must identify the command and support
                relationships for the deploying element to determine who has operational control, tactical control,
                and administrative control over the element while in country.

FUNDING LINES AVAILABLE

SECURITY COOPERATION ORGANIZATION CONDUCTS VETTING OF HOST-NATION FORCES
TO TRAIN

U.S. EMBASSY AND GEOGRAPHIC COMBATANT COMMAND PROVIDE MISSION APPROVAL

          • Originator: Geographic combatant command.

                 Higher headquarters (G3 or S3)

D–240 TO D–210 TASKS

DEPLOYING ELEMENT REVIEWS AND ASSESSES HIGHER HEADQUARTERS OPERATION
ORDER/OPERATION PLAN CONCEPT OF SUPPORT FOR THE MISSION

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing:

                 Higher headquarters (S4)
                 Operational contract support staff.

                documents to determine how sustainment is to be provided during the operation. Based on analysis
                of these plans, planners determine what sustainment gaps exist and may need to be filled by

                purchasing means are linguists, class I, lodging, mobile telephones, Internet, vehicles, drivers, and

DEPLOYING ELEMENT DETERMINES POTENTIAL CONTRACT SUPPORT REQUIREMENTS
AND OPERATIONAL CONTRACT SUPPORT POINTS OF CONTACT

                support or require new contract support for the operation. The operational contract support cell can
                assist the deploying element with the following:
                 Coordinating for contract support that already exists in theater.

                 Providing important points of contact for supporting theater support contracting activities and
                     civil augmentation program support available (for example, logistics civil augmentation
                     program).

                 Understanding capabilities, limitations, and restrictions on use of contracted support.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT DETERMINES IF AN ACSA (ACQUISITION AND CROSS-SERVING
AGREEMENT) IS IN PLACE WITH THE HOST NATION

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing:

                 Higher headquarters (S4 and operational contract support)
                 Geographic combatant command (J8).
          • Remarks: An ACSA allows US personnel to draw support directly from the host nation. The host
                nation is then monetarily reimbursed for this support. This is often the preferred way for Advisory
                forces to draw their support. When advising in combat operations, this may be the ONLY way for
                US Advisors to draw some types of support.
                 If an ACSA is not currently in effect with a host nation, SFA forces should endeavor to set one

                     up as part of their mission. This will significantly ease advising during combat if the mission
                     transitions from competition to crisis/combat, or if the US works with the partner nation to
                     conduct combat operations at any point in the future.
                 At times, the primary strategic goal of a particular SFA mission may simply be to get an ACSA
                     in place to set conditions for possible future combat operations. See FM 3-22 for further details.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT SUBMITS GOVERNMENT TRAVEL CREDIT CARD APPLICATION AND
COMPLETES TRAINING

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S1 Defense Travel System manager).

                to complete and receive cards before departure. All members of the deploying element should apply

                 Any medical emergencies where an out-of-country air evacuation is needed, the unit should

PREDEPLOYMENT SITE SURVEY TEAM SUBMITS PASSPORT/ VISA REQUESTS

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S1 battalion [unit] passport manager).
          • Remarks: The deploying element should plan for (and expect) significant delays during the visa

                application process due to political considerations and potential inefficiencies at host-nation
                embassies and consulates. Some countries may require a letter of introduction that should be
                requested through the supported Embassy security cooperation organization. An approved visa is
                often required for country clearance and can delay the Aircraft and Personnel Automated Clearance
                System approval process. Deploying units should always refer to the Foreign Clearance Guide to
                verify the entry requirements.

HIGHER HEADQUARTERS BEGINS SOURCING FOR SECURITY FORCE ASSISTANCE–SPECIFIC
OR REQUIRED MOVEMENT TRAINING

          • Originator: Higher headquarters (S3).
          • Staffing: Unit and post training management.
          • Remarks: Required training to prepare a security force assistance training team may need to be

                resourced through an outside agency (for example, a mobile training team) or prioritized amongst
                competing home-station units and should be sourced as soon as possible. Planners should refer to
                AR 350-1 and installation –specific training management and resourcing regulations for specific
                requirements and constraints.

D–180 TASKS

GEOGRAPHIC COMBATANT COMMAND ISSUES INITIAL TERMS OF REFERENCE AND
GUIDANCE

          • Originator: Geographic combatant command.
          • Staffing: Deploying element or its higher headquarters (S3 or G3).
          • Remarks: The geographic combatant command issues initial terms of reference and guidance to

                the appropriate Service component command, which submits a request for forces to the Joint Chiefs
                of Staff through the geographic combatant command and the Service. The deploying element is
                designated and issued a warning order, which formalizes its plans for reception, staging, onward
                movement, and integration; base support; communications integration; and sustainment. Deploying
                units facilitate mission planning by conducting predeployment site surveys and subsequent
                coordination for ammunition, air, and logistics.

GEOGRAPHIC COMBATANT COMMAND IDENTIFIES FUNDING AUTHORITIES

          • Originator: Geographic combatant command or higher headquarters (G3 or S3).
          • Staffing:

                 Deploying element
                 Higher headquarters (S3 and S4).
          • Remarks: The Geographic Combatant Command in coordination with the Embassy Security
                Cooperation Office determines if the mission will be done under Title 10 (SFA) or Title 22 (SA)
                authority. The geographic combatant command identifies Title 22 funding authorities under the
                Foreign Assistance Act (if appropriate) and coordinates with the U.S. Embassy and Department of
                State to transfer funds through the Defense Security Cooperation Agency to Service component
                commands. Service component commands execute a military interdepartmental purchase request to
                obtain funding. If under Title 10 authority, Army Service component commands (or a designated
                joint task force) are funded through the established base budget, overseas contingency operations
                funding, or funds otherwise appropriated by Congress. If required, the geographic combatant
                command (in conjunction with the country team) negotiates an acquisition and cross-support
                agreement with the host nation to ensure payment of logistical support for services provided by the
                host nation. Additionally, other bilateral agreements may exist that ensure the availability of
                appropriated host-nation resources. These agreements may include payment to the U.S. through

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  15

Chapter 2

                foreign military sales. Note that if the deployment is funded through Title 22 funds, the mission is
                actually to conduct Security Assistance. If the deployment is funded through Title 10 funds, the
                mission is to conduct Security Force Assistance. For the mission executor on the ground, the
                difference between conducting SA and SFA may be transparent. However, the two different
                Security Cooperation funding sources come with different authorities, restrictions, and
                administrative requirements.

GEOGRAPHIC COMBATANT COMMAND GRANTS DIRECT LIAISON AUTHORITY

          • Originator: Geographic combatant command.
          • Staffing: Deploying element and their higher headquarters.
          • Remarks: The geographic combatant command grants direct liaison authority between the

                deploying element and security cooperation organization or assigns deploying forces to a Service
                component or a joint task force. Additional levels of command may require copies of official traffic
                and coordination.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT SUBMITS PROPOSED PREDEPLOYMENT SITE SURVEY DATES

          • Originator: Geographic combatant command.
          • Staffing:

                 Higher headquarters (S3)
                 Geographic combatant command
                 Security cooperation organization.
          • Remarks: The deploying element submits proposed predeployment site survey dates to the
                geographic combatant command. The proposed dates should include time spent going to and from
                the geographic combatant command headquarters for briefings and guidance, as well as travel to the
                host nation. Planners must consider crossing time zones and delays in foreign countries. Planners
                must anticipate the requirements to obtain official passports and visas. In addition, planners must
                consider whether they will be using commercial aircraft or U.S. military aircraft. Country clearance
                requirements by country are published in the Department of Defense Electronic Foreign Clearance
                Guide (https://www.fcg.pentagon.mil/fcg.cfm). Country clearance is obtained through the Aircraft
                and Personnel Automated Clearance System; this system may be accessed at https://apacs.dtic.mil
                (for unclassified missions and travel) or https://apacs.dtic.smil.mil (for classified missions and
                travel).

DEPLOYING ELEMENT CONFIRMS OPERATIONS ORDER ISSUE FROM GEOGRAPHIC
COMBATANT COMMAND

          • Originator: Deploying element and higher headquarters staff.
          • Staffing: Geographic combatant command.
          • Remarks: It is important for the deploying element to identify issues prior to advance echelon

                departure.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT CONTINUES CONTRACT SUPPORT REQUIREMENTS DEVELOPMENT

          • Originator: Deploying element (requiring activity).
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (operational contracting support staff, S4, and supporting contracting

                activity/ contracting support brigade).
          • Remarks: Requiring activities routinely perform requirements development tasks. Requirements

                development begins with the decision, based on the overall support planning process, that a unit’s
                support requirements (supply, service, or minor construction) will be fulfilled by commercial
                support. General requiring activity requirements development related tasks include the following:
                 Developing contract support requirements packages to include independent government

                     estimate, initial statement of work or performance works statements, any letters of justification,
                     draft quality assurance and surveillance plan for service contracts, and any other supporting
                     documents, as required.
                 Obtaining local staff, command, and funding approval of all requirements packages.

16  TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                 Advisory Mission Pre-deployment Planning and Preparation

                 Nominating technically qualified and process-trained contracting officer representatives for
                     service contracts.

                 Tracking contract support requirements packages through the approval process.
                 If members of the deploying element will be working at the U.S. Embassy or receiving support

                     or services from non-Department of Defense Embassy offices, the Department of Defense may
                     need to reimburse the Department of State for these services using the International Cooperative
                     Administrative Support Services System. These requirements should be discussed with the
                     security cooperation organization and Embassy financial management office during the
                     predeployment site survey.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT SELECTS ADVISORS FOR THE MISSION (GENERAL PURPOSE FORCES
ONLY). SEE APPENDIX A (ADVISOR SCREENING AND SELECTION).

          • Staffing: Higher headquarters
          • Remarks: General purpose forces who are not organized, trained, and equipped for SFA tasks must

                select which members of the unit will serve as advisors for the deployment. Historically, the
                selection of advisors is often the most important task to enable success of an SFA mission. See
                Appendix A, Advisor Screening and Selection, for details on selecting advisors. The selection of
                advisors made at this stage of the process is not final. Following the PDSS (Pre-Deployment Site
                Survey), the deploying unit should update its Advisor selection based on information
                learned/confirmed during the PDSS.

DEPLOYING ADVISORS FAMILIARIZE THEMSELVES WITH APPROVED TACTICS, TECHNIQUES
AND PROCEDURES FOR ADVISING FROM ATP 3-07.10

          • Staffing: Deploying headquarters
          • Remarks: ATP 3-07.10 is a multi-service TTP providing approved tactics, techniques, and

                procedures for advising across all service components.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT COMPLETES ARMY SERVICE COMPONENT COMMAND AND UNIT
REQUIRED TRAINING

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing:

                 Army Service component command
                 Geographic combatant command.
          • Remarks: All regionally aligned forces are required to be trained in their standard Headquarters
                Department of the Army–approved mission-essential task list. Regionally aligned forces in support
                of United Nations or African Union peacekeeping missions receive training from the Peacekeeping
                and Stability Operations Institute. Regionally aligned forces in support of advise-and-assist missions
                receive training from 3-353d Advise and Assist Battalion (Joint Readiness Training Center). (U.S.
                Forces Command Order: Regionally Aligned Forces Training Requirements [111316ZFEB14]).

DEPLOYING ELEMENT COMPLETES BASIC EMBASSY KNOWLEDGE TRAINING

          • Originator: Geographic combatant command/Army Service component command.
          • Staffing: Deploying element.
          • Remarks: The deploying element must ensure Soldiers are trained on basic embassy structure and

                personnel. The country Embassy Web page should provide updated information on personnel and
                activities. Utilize CALL Handbook 23-05-(730), Guide to the US Embassy, as a reference and see
                Appendix I, US Embassy.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT COMPLETES BASIC SECURITY COOPERATION TRAINING

          • Originator: Deploying unit.
          • Staffing: Joint Center for International Security Force Assistance.

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  17


---

Chapter 2

          • Remarks: All deploying leaders should go to Joint Knowledge Online and take the following JKO
                course: Security Force Assistance 101- J3OP-US1336 (1hr). This one-hour course provides an
                excellent overview of Security Cooperation and Security Force Assistance from the strategic down
                to the tactical level.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT REGISTERS FOR SOCIUM ACCOUNTS AND COMPLETE SOCIUM USER

TRAINING

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: DSCU (Defense Security Cooperation University)
          • Remarks: All deploying leaders should have a SOCIUM account. SOCIUM is the web-based

                program of record for all Security Cooperation events. All SFA events must be recorded in
                SOCIUM, to include Assessment, Monitoring, and Evaluation data from the event. SFA
                practitioners must register for a SOCIUM account and take the basic user course. This will enable
                them to record their own SFA activities, as well as refer to past and adjacent SFA events within their
                Area of Interest. See Appendix K (SOCIUM) for detailed instructions.

DEPLOYING ELEMENTS RESEARCH PREVIOUS AND ADJACENT SFA AND SA MISSIONS IN

THEIR AREA OF INTEREST AND COORDINATES AS APPROPRIATE

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Security Cooperation Office for US Embassy in host nation, National Guard SPP partner

                for host nation, regionally aligned SFAB for AOR, TSOC for AOR,, SATMO (Security Assistance
                Training Management Organization).
          • Remarks: Prior to developing a tentative concept for execution of partner training, the deploying
                element research past and ongoing SFA events with the partner nation. Some of these events will
                be in SOCIUM; however, SOCIUM is a recent program of record, and the database is not complete.
                If the deploying element is relieving an outgoing SFA element for a persistent advising mission, the
                outgoing element will have most of the necessary information. If not, the Security Cooperation
                Office within the US Embassy of the Host Nation should have a record of previous SFA events that
                may be related to your mission. Most partner nations also have a SPP (State Partnership Program)
                partner from the National Guard. The SPP partner for that nation will have detailed information on
                the host nation’s military and previous SFA missions, to include monitoring and evaluation
                information from the mission. Additionally, the regionally aligned SFABs may have conducted
                missions with that particular host nation. If so, they will also have information on the host nation
                military and a record of previous SFA missions they have conducted. Last of all, SATMO is the
                organization that manages all SA events for the US Army. These training events are often tied to
                items such as FMS (Foreign Military Sales) and may be contracted training. Understanding
                SATMO-driven training events within the host nation is also important. Gathering all of this
                information is critical to developing a realistic tentative concept of training prior to conducting the
                Initial Assessment on the PDSS.

DEVELOP TENTATIVE CONCEPT(S) OF TRAINING PRIOR TO PDSS

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Security Cooperation Organization
          • Remarks: During the PDSS, leaders from the deploying element will conduct the Initial

                Assessment. Conducting through research and developing tentative concepts for training prior to
                the PDSS will assist in this effort. SFA practitioners must be highly cautioned to not become wed
                to their tentative concept. In all likelihood, it will change significantly during the Initial Assessment.
                However, going into the PDSS with a tentative concept—or multiple tentative concepts, is
                advisable. Note that the tentative plan must consider all congressionally mandated WPS (Women,
                Peace, and Security) requirements as appropriate, IAW Appendix I. Also, see Appendix B, Team
                Leader Checklist, re: concept of training.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT RECEIVES MEDICAL THREAT BRIEF

          • Originator: Medical personnel

---

                 Advisory Mission Pre-deployment Planning and Preparation

          • Staffing: Deploying element
          • Remarks: The deploying element receives pertinent medical threat information and guidance per

                their respective mission, theater, and area of responsibility.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT SUBMITS AMMUNITION REQUEST

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S3 or G3 ammunition manager).
          • Remarks: Request should be submitted not later than 180 days prior to the draw date. Planners must

                identify ammunition type by Department of Defense Identification Code. Ammunition required will
                be from two categories: 1) ammunition required to conduct the host-nation security force assistance
                training, and 2) force protection ammunition, which requires a separate request for each category.
                Deploying element should coordinate with the Department of Defense office to ensure that host-
                nation–specific ammunition restrictions and requirements are considered.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT SUBMITS AMMUNITION PURCHASE REQUEST

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S3).
          • Remarks: The deploying element should submit this request if Title 10 ammunition is required (that

                is, if the unit is not utilizing preprogrammed training ammunition).

PREDEPLOYMENT SITE SURVEY TEAM COMPLETES THEATER/COUNTRY CLEARANCE
REQUIRED TRAINING

          • Originator: Predeployment site survey team and deploying element.
          • Staffing: Battalion (unit) passport manager and S3.
          • Remarks: Planners should reference the mission execution order or deployment order for specific

                training requirements. The predeployment site survey team must adhere to the strict guidelines
                described in Department of Defense Electronic Foreign Clearance Guide
                (https://www.fcg.pentagon.mil/fcg.cfm ) Theater/country clearance requires the following:
                Antiterrorism/Force Protection Level 1 training, an updated DD Form 1833, Survival, Evasion,
                Resistance, and Escape Level 100 training; human rights class; updated passport and visa; theater-
                required vaccinations and yellow shot card; and itinerary.

HIGHER HEADQUARTERS PROVIDES TRAVEL ITINERARY FOR PREDEPLOYMENT SITE
SURVEY TEAM

          • Originator: Higher headquarters (S3).
          • Staffing: Deploying element, geographic combatant command, security cooperation organization.
          • Remarks: Itinerary dates should include time spent going to and from the geographic combatant

                command headquarters for briefings, guidance, and so on, and travel to the host nation. The
                predeployment site survey team should refer to the geographic combatant command deployment
                requirements standard operating procedures for additional information.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT SUBMITS PASSPORT/VISA REQUESTS FOR MAIN BODY

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S1 battalion [unit] passport manager).
          • Remarks: The deploying element should expect and plan for significant delays during the visa

                application process due to political considerations and potential inefficiencies at host nation
                embassies and consulates. Some countries may require a letter of introduction that should be
                requested through the supported Embassy Department of Defense office.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT SUBMITS CUSTOMS FORMS, DIPLOMATIC NOTE, COPY OF DENSITY
LIST

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing:

Chapter 2

                 Higher headquarters (S2 and S3)
                 Geographic combatant command.
          • Remarks: Units should contact the geographic combatant command or refer to the deployment
                order for additional guidance.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT COORDINATES WITH UNIT PASSPORT MANAGER

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S1 or unit passport manager).
          • Remarks: The deploying element is responsible for maintaining copies of all official passports and

                expiration dates. Additional information may be found in the unit standard operating procedures.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT COORDINATES MOVEMENT SUPPORT

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing:

                 Higher headquarters (S4 or G4)
                 Installation transportation office.
          • Remarks: The deploying element and higher headquarters requests movement support from their
                installation transportation office. The installation transportation office is the coordinating agency
                for military and commercial aircraft and ships. The installation transportation office coordinates
                movement to the aerial port of debarkation if the installation does not have a collocated airfield.
                CJCSI 4120.02C provides additional information regarding movement priority for specific mission
                types. Deploying elements and headquarters should refer to the Defense Transportation Regulation
                to ensure that coordinated transportation plans comply with applicable regulatory requirements.

HIGHER HEADQUARTERS VERIFIES AMMUNITION HANDLER CERTIFICATION

          • Originator: Higher headquarters (S3).
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S3).
          • Remarks: The deploying element must have personnel that are certified for movement of supplies

                and equipment. Planners should refer to the unit standard operating procedure and check for
                installation availability.

HIGHER HEADQUARTERS VERIFIES HAZARDOUS MATERIAL CERTIFICATION

          • Originator: Higher headquarters (S3).
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S3).
          • Remarks: The deploying element must have personnel that are hazardous material certified for

                movement of supplies and equipment. Planners should refer to the unit standard operating procedure
                and check for installation availability.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT IDENTIFIES STATUS OF FORCES AGREEMENT (IF APPLICABLE)

          • Originator: Higher headquarters (staff judge advocate).
          • Staffing: Not applicable.
          • Remarks: Specific restrictions or requirements of the status of-forces agreement will inform

                training and affect administrative and logistical requirements. The unit staff judge advocate should
                brief deploying unit leadership on specific legal protections provided by the status-of-forces
                agreement or other agreements between the U.S. Government and the host nation. If there is no
                SOFA, the US Embassy, through the Security Cooperation Office, can provide information on
                existing Diplomatic Notes that are fulfilling that function.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT AND HIGHER HEADQUARTERS COORDINATE WITH EMBASSY
DEFENSE ATTACHÉ OFFICE AND SECURITY COOPERATION ORGANIZATION

          • Originator: Deploying element and higher headquarters (S3).
          • Staffing: Geographic combatant command.

20  TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                 Advisory Mission Pre-deployment Planning and Preparation

          • Remarks: The deploying element should contact the U.S. Embassy security cooperation
                organization to coordinate the conduct of the predeployment site survey and verify country clearance
                requirements for predeployment site survey team– to include team composition, mission, timeline
                and desired itinerary, and tasks. The Embassy security cooperation organization will coordinate to
                support the predeployment site survey and ensure that the senior defense official, ambassador, and
                country team are aware of and concur with the predeployment site survey prior to execution. The
                security cooperation organization schedules required protocol and briefings at the Embassy and with
                the host nation.

PREDEPLOYMENT SITE SURVEY TEAM SUBMITS THEATER/COUNTRY CLEARANCE
REQUESTS USING THE AIRCRAFT AND Personnel Automated Clearance System

          • Originator: Predeployment site survey element or higher headquarters (S-3).
          • Staffing:

                 Higher headquarters (S3 or G3)
                 Geographic combatant command
                 Security cooperation organization.
          • Remarks: The Aircraft and Personnel Automated Clearance System is a Web-based tool designed
                to aid Department of Defense travelers on official business overseas in meeting the clearance
                requirements outlined in the Department of Defense Foreign Clearance Guide. The Aircraft and
                Personnel Automated Clearance System can be accessed using a Department of Defense common
                access card at https://apacs.dtic.mil. The Aircraft and Personnel Automated Clearance System
                automates the process of requesting and approving diplomatic and personnel clearances through a
                common, centralized, and secure database. The deploying element team must adhere to the strict
                guidelines described in Department of Defense Electronic Foreign Clearance Guide
                (https://www.fcg.pentagon.mil/fcg.cfm). Depending on unit or supported command standard
                operating procedures, the deploying element, higher headquarters or supported command will
                submit the Aircraft and Personnel Automated Clearance System itinerary for the deploying element
                to obtain theater and country clearance prior to travel. The Aircraft and Personnel Automated
                Clearance System requests must be submitted not later than 30 days prior to departure. The
                deploying element should contact their geographic combatant command and security cooperation
                organization points of contact prior to submitting an Aircraft and Personnel Automated Clearance
                System request to ensure local policies and procedures are followed and to facilitate rapid
                processing.

                main-body arrival.

          • Originator: Predeployment site survey team.
          • Staffing:

                 Security cooperation organization
                 Geographic combatant command
                 Higher headquarters (G3 or S3).
          • Remarks: The memorandum of agreement outlines the terms agreed upon by all parties involved
                in the training, providers of resource requirements, and outcome expectations with direct assistance
                from security cooperation organization. The memorandum of agreement should be distributed to the
                host nation unit and higher headquarters by the security cooperation organization and to the
                geographic combatant command, Army Service component command, and deploying unit higher
                headquarters by the predeployment site survey team. Depending on the mission type, size, and
                scope, the deploying unit may support an existing agreement or an ongoing engagement. In this
                case, the predeployment site survey team must confirm training and resource requirements for which
                they will be responsible during the deployment.

Chapter 2

          • Remarks: If the deploying element is using a new landing strip for training, the U.S. Air Force will
