GEOGRAPHIC COMBATANT COMMAND DISPENSES MONIES ALLOCATED TO MISSION

          • Originator: Geographic combatant command.
          • Staffing:

                 Higher headquarters (G3 or S3)
                 Deploying element.
          • Remarks: The geographic combatant command identifies and disseminates the amount of money
                allocated to mission budget and constraints. Funding may include more than one title authority (that
                is, more than one “pot of money” to resource the mission), title funding authority, and amount
                allocated. The Army Service component command identifies resources required to fund basing and
                operations in conjunction with security force assistance. The Army Service component command
                apportions resources out of on-hand operations and maintenance funding and accounts for
                expenditures. Those apportioned funds are derived from existing operations and maintenance funds
                or overseas contingency operations funds until such time that the mission becomes a named
                operation, and then funds are appropriated for the mission by Congress under Title 10. The
                geographic combatant command identifies and disseminates any on-hand funds it wishes to use from
                funds previously apportioned through the Defense Security Cooperation Agency in conjunction with
                humanitarian assistance programs, counterterrorism programs, or commanders’ initiative funds that
                may contribute to the foreign humanitarian assistance mission.

GEOGRAPHIC COMBATANT COMMAND ASSIGNS A MISSION TRACKING NUMBER

          • Originator: Geographic combatant command.
          • Staffing:

                 Higher headquarters (G3 or S3)
                 Deploying element.
          • Remarks: The geographic combatant command assigns a mission tracking number. This number is
                used for all requests for information, requests for supplies, and for mission tracking purposes on
                unclassified media.

HIGHER HEADQUARTERS UPDATES AND CONTINUES INTELLIGENCE PREPARATION

          • Originator: Higher headquarters (S2).
          • Staffing: Deploying element.
          • Remarks: This data is used to plan and develop the personnel recovery plan, threat assessments,

                briefs, and so on.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT SUBMITS DETAILED BUDGET ESTIMATE FOR APPROVAL

          • Originator: Deploying element or higher headquarters (S3 and S4).
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S4 and S3 G4 and G3).
          • Remarks: Upon completion of the predeployment site survey, the deploying element identifies

                ammunition, air, transportation, life-support, contracts, Embassy services (requiring International
                Cooperative Administrative Support Services billing), and operational fund costs to be within
                budget. Planners should use unit or geographic combatant command provided formats, title funding
                authority, and amount allocated.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT DEVELOPS INITIAL MISSION TRAINING STRATEGY AND IDENTIFIES
RESOURCE REQUIREMENTS

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S3, S4, and S6, as required).
          • Remarks: The deploying element develops the mission training strategy based on the memorandum

                of agreement developed by the host nation and the geographic combatant command (for example,
                medical, sniper operations, intelligence collection, site exploitation, and host-nation legality). Next,
                planners must identify training venues and durations and then input costs for resources into the

                budget estimate. The deploying element should report any potential budget shortfalls through the

DEPLOYING ELEMENT OBTAINS INITIAL MISSION TRAINING STRATEGY FROM THE ARMY
SERVICE COMPONENT COMMAND OR DESIGNATED JOINT COMMAND AND IDENTIFIES
RESOURCES

          • Originator: Army Service component command.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S3, S4, and S6, as required).
          • Remarks: The mission training strategy must be based on the memorandum of agreement

                developed by the host nation and the geographic combatant command (for example, medical, sniper
                operations, intelligence collection, site exploitation, and host-nation legality). The Army Service
                component command and deploying unit estimate basing requirements to inform the geographic
                combatant command effort to obtain land-use agreements from the host nation. A Service
                component is designated as the lead Service and base operating support integrator for the area of
                operations. The lead Service and base operating support integrator establishes basing and assumes
                costs.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT IDENTIFIES NON–MODIFIED TABLE OF ORGANIZATION AND
EQUIPMENT ITEMS, TO INCLUDE TIME TO TRAIN ON EQUIPMENT

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S3).
          • Remarks: The deploying element identifies equipment and training limitations based upon the

                predeployment site survey and coordination with the security cooperation organization.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT CROSSCHECKS THE MISSION TRAINING PLAN AGAINST THE
MISSION-ESSENTIAL TASK LIST AND HOST-NATION MISSION REQUIREMENTS

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S3 or G3) Geographic combatant command.
          • Remarks: Planners must develop programs of instruction that meet mission requirements, measures

                of performance, and measures of effectiveness that identify whether or not the training is progressing
                and the effectiveness of the training. During the predeployment site survey, the deploying element
                should discuss programs of instruction with host-nation commanders and leaders to gain perspective
                and elicit input.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT CONDUCTS IN-PROGRESS REVIEWS AND APPROVAL BRIEFS WITH
PARENT UNIT AND GEOGRAPHIC COMBATANT COMMAND AS REQUIRED

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S3 or G3) Geographic combatant command.
          • Remarks: The deploying element requests briefing formats and requirements from parent unit and

                geographic combatant command.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT BEGINS FORCE PROTECTION PLANNING AND IDENTIFIES RESOURCE
AND INTELLIGENCE GAPS

          • Originator:
                 Deploying element higher headquarters (S2 and S4 or G2 and -4)
                 Geographic combatant command.

          • Staffing: Geographic combatant command and Security cooperation organization.
          • Remarks: The deploying element uses information from the predeployment site survey, open

                sources, the U.S. embassy (regional security officer) threat assessment, and a site vulnerability
                assessment to develop a force protection plan for locations, route selection, and insider threat
                prevention. (See Appendix E, Force Protection, for more details).

Chapter 2

HIGHER HEADQUARTERS DEVELOPS, COORDINATES, AND IMPLEMENTS PREDEPLOYMENT
TRAINING FOR DEPLOYING ELEMENT

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters, as required.
          • Remarks: The deploying element conducts regional, cultural, basic language, individual and

                collective tasks, rapport building, medical, and engagement skills training according to the
                deployment training guidance. Planners should refer to mission execution or deployment order for
                additional guidance.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT CONTINUES MOVEMENT COORDINATION

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing:

                 Higher headquarters (G3 and Joint Operation Planning and Execution System manager)
                 Installation transportation office.
          • Remarks: Not later than D–90, the Unit Deployment List is submitted with Level 6 data. The Unit
                Deployment List is transmitted to the Computerized Movements Planning and Status System and
                submitted to Joint Operation Planning and Execution System. This also serves as the strategic lift
                request.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT IDENTIFIES SUSTAINMENT GAPS AND SUBMITS INITIAL REQUEST
FOR SUPPORT

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (-3 and S4 or G3 and G4).
          • Remarks: Support requests should include Class I through Class X supplies, as well as military

                occupation specialty– specific attachments based upon mission requirements. Support requests
                should be according to mission terms of reference and memoranda of agreement support by the
                geographic combatant command.

D–90 TASKS

DEPLOYING ELEMENT SUBMITS OPERATIONAL FUNDING REQUEST

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S4 and comptroller).
          • Remarks: Operational funding is used for items and services that cannot be obtained prior to

                deployment or to replenish expendable items used during the deployment and training. To the
                greatest extent possible, the deploying element should acquire needed mission supplies prior to
                deployment, as availability in the host nation may be limited or nonexistent. The predeployment site
                survey personnel can inquire and reconnoiter sources and types of supplies available.

HIGHER HEADQUARTERS SUBMITS COMMUNICATIONS SECURITY REQUESTS

          • Originator: Higher headquarters (S6).
          • Staffing: Geographic combatant command G6.
          • Remarks: The deploying element requests communication information and guidance and submits

                required unit communications information according to geographic combatant command guidance.
                Planners should coordinate with the Embassy for classified and sensitive information policies and
                storage capabilities. Specific local procedures and expectations for communications security storage
                and control should be discussed with the defense attaché office during the predeployment site
                survey.

HAZARDOUS MATERIAL CERTIFICATION AND PALLETIZATION

          • Originator: Higher headquarters (S3).
          • Staffing: Ammunition supply point.
          • Remarks: Planners must submit required documents (electronic DA Form 581 [Request for issue

                and Turn-On of Ammunition], signature cards, and so on).

DEPLOYING ELEMENT BEGINS INSTRUCTOR TRAINING

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing:

                 Geographic combatant command.
          • Remarks: Leaders and Soldiers identified as instructors and trainers attend required certification
                courses or unit designated training according to command guidance.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT SUBMITS PERSONNEL SUPPORT REQUEST (PROCESSING FOR
OVERSEAS MOVEMENT AND SOLDIER READINESS PROCESSING)

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S1 or G1).
          • Remarks: Processing is conducted as required by the command.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT CONDUCTS MEDICAL TASKS

          • Originator: Medical personnel.
          • Staffing: Deploying element.
          • Remarks: Requirements and tasks should be provided according to execution or deployment order

                guidance and should include required medical training, vaccinations, and medical evacuation
                planning.

HIGHER HEADQUARTERS PREPARES LEGAL DOCUMENTS IN ANTICIPATION OF
DEPLOYMENT

          • Originator: Judge advocate general and higher headquarters.
          • Staffing: Deploying element (individual Soldiers).
          • Remarks: Common legal issues include wills, powers of attorney, and Servicemembers Civil Relief

                Act (50 USC 501) matters (involving leases, contracts, and so on). Processing of these documents
                can be completed at a Soldier readiness center, and group appointments may be possible for
                deploying personnel.

HIGHER HEADQUARTERS GATHERS MEDICAL INTELLIGENCE AND SUBMITS REQUESTS
FOR INFORMATION TO SUPPORT THE MISSION

          • Originator: Higher headquarters (medical staff and S2).
          • Staffing: Deploying element.
          • Remarks: Medical intelligence is required to order and develop medical supplies (Class VIII), force

                health protection items, preventive medical supplies, medications, and packing lists. The medical
                staff should identify antivenom storage requirements or bite-kit requirements and identify all
                approved host-nation hospitals, blood supplies, and approved supply vendors. Additional
                information is available at the National Center for Medical Intelligence at:

                      https://www.ncmi.detrick.army.mil.

MEDICAL TREATMENT FACILITY OR HIGHER HEADQUARTERS AND DEPLOYING ELEMENT
EMPLOY PRESCRIPTION MEDICATION ANALYSIS AND REPORTING TOOL IN CONJUNCTION
WITH HOSPITAL PHARMACY FOR THE ENTIRE UNIT

          • Originator: Medical treatment facility and higher headquarters (medical staff).

Chapter 2

          • Staffing: Deploying element and medical personnel
          • Remarks: The Medical Protection System is used to identify nondeployable personnel.

                https://medpros.mods.army.mil/MEDPROSNew/.

HIGHER HEADQUARTERS ANALYZES PREDEPLOYMENT SITE SURVEY INFORMATION,
DEVELOPS MEDICAL CONTINGENCIES, AND RESOURCES MEDICAL SUPPLIES (CLASS VIII)

          • Originator: Higher headquarters (medical staff).
          • Staffing: Deploying element.
          • Remarks: This information is required to develop the medical concept of support and to order

                preventive medical supplies and medications. Planners must identify medical rules of engagement
                and emergency medical procedures in country or in adjacent countries to preserve life, limb, or
                eyesight for U.S. personnel and host-nation personnel. Planners should refer to the predeployment
                site survey trip report.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT SUBMITS DENSITY LIST

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing:

                 Deploying element
                 Security cooperation organization
          • Remarks: This list must account for all high-dollar and sensitive items going on the deployment.
                The list must be extremely accurate by national stock number and serial number. The deploying
                element must report any changes as soon as possible to accurately track the density list and gain
                approval for items that may be unlawful or considered contraband. Some countries may require a
                list of items that the host nation considers contraband. If the deploying element did not inform the
                appropriate host-nation authorities and receive approval prior to entering the country, the host nation
                may confiscate (nationalize) specific items. Any changes must be reported as soon as possible and
                accurately tracked.

D–60 TASKS

DEPLOYING ELEMENT SUBMITS HAZARDOUS MATERIAL AND DIPLOMATIC SHEET LOAD
PLAN AND MANIFEST

          • Originator: Deploying element and higher headquarters (-4 and S3).
          • Staffing: Higher headquarters (S3 air operations) and installation transportation office.
          • Remarks: The deploying element submits a hazardous materials listing and provides the

                information needed by supporting units, the country team, and appropriate host nation personnel to
                plan, prepare, approve, and fully support the deploying element. The diplomatic aircraft sheet and
                load plan require this information to accurately plan and prepare the requisite documents. If the
                deploying element does not provide accurate information to the country team or aircrew, the host
                nation may not allow the aircraft to land or offload, and the host nation may confiscate deployment
                items.

HIGHER HEADQUARTERS SUBMITS MILITARY INTERDEPARTMENTAL PURCHASE REQUEST
FUNDS TO U.S. EMBASSY FOR CONTRACT SUPPORT (IF REQUIRED) OR INTERNATIONAL
COOPERATIVE ADMINISTRATIVE SUPPORT SERVICES MEMORANDUM OF UNDERSTANDING
FOR U.S. EMBASSY SERVICES AND SUPPORT

          • Originator: Higher headquarters or geographic combatant command (G8).
          • Staffing: U.S. Embassy.
          • Remarks: The higher headquarters or geographic combatant command comptroller sends funds to

                the U.S. Embassy contracting office for contract support under existing or newly awarded contracts.
                It should be noted that under a 2008 Department of State memorandum, the Department of State is
                no longer required to process contracts for the Department of Defense. Therefore, depending on
                Embassy-specific policies, the geographic combatant command or Army Service component

                                  Advisory Mission Pre-deployment Planning and Preparation

command contracting offices or contracting brigades may be directly responsible for contracts
related to the security force assistance mission. If members of the deploying element will work

services, the geographic combatant command or Army Service component command may need to
pay for these services using the International Cooperative Administrative Support Services System.
This requires a memorandum of understanding between the geographic combatant command or
Army Service component command and the Embassy finance office. International Cooperative
Administrative Support Services System requirements should be discussed during the
predeployment site survey.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT IDENTIFIES FIELD ORDERING OFFICER AND PAYING AGENT AND

     isolated personnel report card; Survival, Evasion, Resistance, and Escape Level 100 training;
     human-rights class; updated passport and visa; and itinerary. Planners should refer to Survival,
     Evasion, Resistance, and Escape Level 100 training at
     http://jko.jten.mil/references/SERE_tip%20sheet_jun%202013.pdf.

HIGHER HEADQUARTERS VALIDATES COMPLETION OF ADVANCED ECHELON AND MAIN
BODY THEATER/ COUNTRY CLEARANCE REQUIREMENTS

          • Originator: Higher headquarters (S3).

                 Security cooperation organization.
          • Remarks: The deploying element must adhere to the strict guidelines described in Department of
                Defense Electronic Foreign Clearance Guide (https://www.fcg.pentagon.mil/fcg.cfm).
                Theater/country clearance requires the following: Antiterrorism/Force Protection Level 1 training,
                an updated DD Form 1833; Survival, Evasion, Resistance, and Escape Level 100 training; human
                rights class; updated passport and visa; theater-required vaccinations and yellow shot card; and
                itinerary.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT SUBMITS ADVANCED ECHELON AND MAIN BODY
THEATER/COUNTRY CLEARANCE REQUESTS USING THE AIRCRAFT AND PERSONNEL
AUTOMATED CLEARANCE SYSTEM

          • Originator: Deploying element or higher headquarters (S3).
          • Staffing:

                 Higher headquarters (S3 or G3)
                 Geographic combatant command
                 Security cooperation organization.
          • Remarks: The Aircraft and Personnel Automated Clearance System is a Web-based tool designed
                to aid Department of Defense travelers on official business overseas in meeting the clearance
                requirements outlined in the Department of Defense Foreign Clearance Guide. The Aircraft and
                Personnel Automated Clearance System automates the process of requesting and approving
                diplomatic and personnel clearances through a common, centralized, and secure database. The
                deploying element team must adhere to the strict guidelines described in Department of Defense
                Electronic Foreign Clearance Guide Depending upon unit or supported command standard operating
                procedures, the deploying element, higher headquarters, or supported command submits the Aircraft
                and Personnel Automated Clearance System itinerary for the deploying element to obtain theater
                and country clearance prior to travel. Aircraft and Personnel Automated Clearance System requests
                must be submitted not later than 30 days prior to departure. The deploying element should contact
                their geographic combatant command and security cooperation organization points of contact prior
                to submitting an Aircraft and Personnel Automated Clearance System request to ensure local
                policies and procedures are followed and to facilitate rapid processing.

                Soldiers that passed processing for overseas movement/Soldier readiness processing.

SECTION IV — ADVISOR PREDEPLOYMENT SITE SURVEY (PDSS)

    Site surveys are critical parts of the pre-mission planning and coordination process for
    persistent and episodic deployments. See Appendix C for a PDSS checklist.

PDSS CHECKLIST

     2-22. Preparation. Prior to deploying, the team leader and selected members of the advisor team may have
     an opportunity to visit the area of operations (AO). Ideally, the PDSS is scheduled 180–270 days prior to
     beginning the mission and is an extremely useful tool to help the new advisor team understand the operational
     environment. A PDSS checklist can help answer many questions regarding foreign security forces (FSF) in
     the area and shortcomings or opportunities to focus advisor efforts for the incoming team. If available,
     incoming teams should consider conducting video teleconferences or gaining access to web portals
     containing current information.

     2-23. Every advisor team will have differing missions, AOs, units, and personalities with which they will
     engage. The environment, relationships, FSF level of autonomy, and level of stability within the AO help
     define their mission.

     2-24. Prior to departure for the PDSS, the incoming team leader should assemble the request for information
     list based on input from the team. The checklist in Appendix K of this Training Circular provides a basic
     outline. Team members involved in developing the PDSS may use the checklist as a guide when preparing
     their PDSS plan.

     2-25. However, it is important for the incoming advisor team to remain flexible and keep an open mind with
     respect to training and operating with their assigned FSF.

     2-26. Site Survey Checklist. The site survey checklist helps answer questions identified by the team during
     its preparation for deployment. The checklist can be modified to aid the site survey team in acquiring
     information for planning before deployment.

40  TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                 Advisory Mission Pre-deployment Planning and Preparation

SITE SURVEY TEAM MISSION

     2-27. The mission of the site survey team is to report on the current FSF mission, enemy, terrain and weather,
     troops and support available, time available, and civil considerations conditions. It also clarifies in-country
     command and control (C2) systems and logistics relationships for the follow-on unit’s mission execution and
     coordinates the in-country reception of the main body. If possible, the PDSS should be conducted by
     members of the actual advisor team in conjunction with the partner force.

SITE SURVEY TEAM PROCEDURES

     2-28. Before departure, the site survey team conducts predeployment activities. Unit standard operating
     procedures should be used when conducting predeployment activities; however, the following lists suggested
     activities:

          • Obtaining required travel documents (i.e., visas and passports) through the unit’s personnel staff
                and a copy of the country clearance messages sent by the United States (US) embassy, if required,
                through the unit’s operations staff and the country briefing through the appropriate intelligence
                office.

          • Ensuring site survey team members’ medical and immunization records are current.
          • Determining financial management support (e.g., the location of supporting finance unit funding).
          • Confirming all agencies concerned with the site survey have been briefed on the team’s itinerary

                and are available for coordination.
          • Receiving and updating the threat briefing and reviewing the rules of engagement (ROE) and status-

                of-forces agreement (SOFA).
          • Conducting a mission analysis and back brief in accordance with unit standard operating procedures.

                The team tailors its mission analysis and back brief to the site survey mission.
          • Assess deployment location to identify environmental threats and hazards to obtain appropriate

                equipment (mosquito nets, treated uniforms, iodine tablets, additional canteens) to mitigate risk to
                the team.
          • Upon arrival in the AO, the team processes through customs, notifies the security cooperation (SC)
                officer of its arrival and status, and requests an updated threat briefing. The survey team must be
                ready to brief the mission and program of instruction to the SC officer for approval or modification.
                 The PDSS team plans its security according to the anticipated threat. Adjustments are made as

                     required by the situation on the ground. The PDSS team members—
                 Fortify their positions (quarters, communications, medical, command) according to the

                     available means and requirements to maintain low visibility.
                 Maintain a team internal guard system—aware of the locations of all other SF advisors—and

                     be ready to react to an emergency by following the alert plan and starting defensive actions.
                 Maintain a team internal alert plan that will notify all team members of an emergency.
                 Maintain communications with all subordinate team members deployed outside of the

                     immediate area controlled by the team.
                 Establish plans for immediate team defensive actions in the event of an insurgent or terrorist

                     attack or a loss of HN rapport with hostile reaction.
                 Discuss visible team security measures with HN counterparts to ensure their understanding and

                     to maintain effective rapport.
                 Encourage the HN unit, through counterparts, to adopt additional security measures that have

                     been identified as necessary during the analysis of the HN unit status and the inspection of its
                     facilities.
                 Establish mutual plans with the HN unit, through counterparts, for defensive actions in the event
                     of an insurgent or terrorist attack.
                 Rehearse team alert and defensive plans.
                 Encourage the HN unit, through counterparts, to conduct mutual, full-force rehearsals of
                     defensive plans.
          • The survey team commander and operations staff confirm the team’s command relationship
                (COMREL) with the next higher, in-country, US commander. The team commander also briefs that
                commander, if requested, on the planned execution of the survey and the required preparations for
                the main body.

Chapter 2

          • The survey team commander obtains any additional guidance from the country team. At a minimum,
                the commander meets with the SC officer to confirm the ROE, evasion and recovery support, and
                any limitations on the relationships with FSF counterparts. The survey team commander discusses
                the following topics with the SC officer.
                 Improvements to the task organization to maximize the strengths and minimize the weaknesses
                     of the available assets, counter the anticipated threat, allow for swift transitions in the
                     organization for contingencies, and maintain a reserve appropriate to the size of the HN force
                     employed.
                 Improvements to the intelligence portions of the plan or order so that all relevant sources of
                     information have been exploited and the information has been analyzed to allow the
                 HN commander to plan actions that seize the initiative.
                 Improvements to the execution so that the mobility of the HN unit is employed to achieve all
                     possible tactical advantage, the minimum firepower needed is used to accomplish the given
                     tasks, restrictions on the rules of engagement are specified, and subunit missions (to include the
                     reserves) are clear.
                 Improvements to the service and support plans so that only mission-essential supplies and
                     equipment are taken, that Soldiers are not overburdened at the expense of the mobility, and that
                     resupply and medical evacuations are available as needed.
                 Any other improvements needed to ensure the OPLAN or OPORD is complete, to include
                     human-rights considerations, measures, or required training.

TRAINING OBJECTIVES

     2-29. Initial terms of reference. (This is captured in a formal document that includes the initial, agreed upon
     training objectives and support requirements for the advisor and the FSF.)

          • Terms of reference (a final, signed agreement).
          • The political situation.
          • Social customs.
          • Guidelines for official and personal associations with foreign personnel.
          • Currency control.
          • Procedures for obtaining intelligence support from the next higher operational control commander.
          • Administrative support.
          • Legal status in relation to the foreign country (i.e., SOFA).
          • Procedures for obtaining logistics from the next higher, in-country US commander, if applicable
          • If the Leahy Amendment Vetting of Foreign Forces has been completed.
          • If country clearance requirements are met.
          • If foreign disclosure requirements will be met.

     2-30. The team commander confirms communications and reporting procedures between the country team
     or the next higher, in-country, US commander, the survey team, and the follow-on advisor units still in
     mission preparation. The team commander, also, must verify the availability of communications equipment
     needed to support the mission.

     2-31. The team commander confirms procedures for obtaining logistics based on the team’s mission orders
     and COMREL. Support may be from the next higher, in- country, US commander depending on COMREL
     or other logistical sources. The team commander identifies a point of contact (POC) at the country team crisis
     management element or at the emergency operations center of the US military staff. The POC then informs
     the team of necessary actions during increased threats or emergencies requiring evacuation of US personnel
     from the AO. The team commander also coordinates procedures to obtain intelligence support from the
     country team, the next higher, in-country, US commander, or other US agencies.

     2-32. The survey team establishes direct working relationships with its next higher, in- country headquarters,
     the country team, or the out-of-country support element. The survey team:

          • Identifies the supporting element’s location.
          • Contacts the supporting element to determine any limitations on support.
          • The team should know the expected reaction time between the initiation of the support request and

                its fulfillment.

                                                                 Advisory Mission Pre-deployment Planning and Preparation

          • Requests support for the in-country reception of the main body in accordance with the requirements
                in the survey team operation orders.

          • Confirms or establishes communications procedures among the supporting element, survey team,
                and follow-on advisor team.

          • They identify alternate and emergency communications procedures for C2, logistics, and medical
                or casualty evacuation.

          • Determines the established communications support requesting procedures for the follow-on advisor
                team.

          • The survey team establishes procedures to promote interagency cooperation and synchronize
                operations. The team:

          • Identifies the location of appropriate FSF and international or US agencies. Contacts the appropriate
                agencies to establish initial coordination.

          • Exchanges information and intelligence.
          • Confirms or establishes communications procedures.
          • Reports the newly established or changed procedures for inclusion in the follow-on advisor mission.
          • The survey team commander establishes a direct working relationship and rapport with the FSF

                commander.

THE SURVEY TEAM

          • Briefs the FSF commander on the advisor team survey mission and the restrictions and limitations
                imposed on the advisor team by the higher US commander.

          • Assures the FSF commander the commander’s assistance is required to develop the tentative
                objectives for training and advisor assistance.

          • Deduces or solicits the FSF commander’s estimate of the unit’s capabilities, required training, and
                advisor assistance, including material requirements. The team discusses training (i.e., plans, current
                status, units available, facilities, and any additional requirements).

          • The survey team obtains the FSF commander’s approval of the plan in accordance with the partner
                nation’s (PN’s) security force development plan and requests a linkup with their counterparts under
                the mutual supervision of the FSF commander and survey team commander.

          • The team does not make any promises (or statements that could be construed as promises) to the
                FSF commander about commitments to provide assistance or fulfill material requirements. In
                particular, the survey team does not:
                 Make any comment to the host government concerning US Government resources of any kind.
                 Provide any kind of independent assessment or confirmation of the external threat as perceived
                     by the FSF.
                 Provide advice on tactics, doctrine, basing, combat planning, or operations.
                 The survey team analyzes the FSF unit’s status to determine its requirements for advisor
                     assistance. The team:
                 Collects enough information to confirm the validity of current intelligence and selects tentative
                     training and advisor assistance courses of action (COAs).
                 Prepares written estimates for advisor assistance COAs prioritized in order of desirability.
                 Analyzes the FSF location and its effects on the populace.
                 Collects and analyzes all information affecting force protection.
                 Determines FSF existing logistics and maintenance support shortfalls and capabilities.
                 Determines the compatibility of recommended US equipment with equipment in the FSF
                     inventory.

     2-33. The survey team helps the FSF unit prepare facilities (i.e., training, security, and administration) for
     the execution of the mission. The survey team inspects the facilities the FSF unit has designated for its use
     during the mission. The team identifies any deficiencies that will prevent execution of the advisor assistance
     COAs. After the inspection, the survey team commander recommends to the FSF commander the most
     desirable COAs to correct any deficiencies found. The survey team commander:

          • Ensures the FSF commander understands the desired COAs are tentative (contingent on the US
                commander’s decision).

          • Ensures the higher, in-country, US commander and country team are informed of significant
                findings in the survey of the FSF unit.

          • Provides recommended COAs to the follow-on advisor team after obtaining input from the FSF
                commander.

          • The survey team always ensures its security according to the latest threat assessment. The team:
          • Fortifies its position (i.e., quarters, communications, medical spaces, and command), using available

                means, keeping in mind the requirement to maintain low visibility.
          • Establishes and maintains an internal alert plan.
          • Organizes and maintains an internal guard system with at least one member who is awake and knows

          • Maintains communication with all team members outside the immediate area occupied by the team’s
                main body.
          • Before departing from the AO, the survey team visits all appropriate US and FSF staff agencies and
                identifies any unresolved problem areas.

                                            Chapter 3

                  Advisory Mission Execution

This chapter provides information on the elements essential to successful execution of advisory
missions. The chapter provides a template to enable advisors to understand and address unique
challenges and develop comprehensive and resilient mission strategies. However, it must be
noted that advisory mission execution will vary based on the specific mission context,
geopolitical environment, and changing nature of international relations and military
operations. Therefore, application of this template must be tailored to each advisory mission's
unique circumstances. Like previous chapters, this chapter is written primarily for units
deploying in competition. However, this chapter also spends significant time highlighting key
considerations for crisis and conflict.

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  45


---

Chapter 3

                US Advisors in Korea before the start of the Korean War

          The experience of the US Advisors in Korea at the start of the Korean War is highly
          instructive on what could happen during a surprise transition from competition to
          conflict. In this historical example, a small US advisor force had been working with the
          Republic of Korea (ROK) Army for several years when the North Korean People’s Army
          (NKPA) surged across the border in June of 1950. The US advisors immediately found
          themselves in a desperate fight for personal survival as they were overrun. Guidance
          to fight with and support the ROK forces came relatively quickly as the KMAG advisors
          desperately worked to establish communication with each other and with their ROKA
          counterparts. This resulted in several noteworthy successes, as well as several
          noteworthy failures. For example, LTC Thomas McPhail’s advisory actions with the
          ROKA 6th Division significantly delayed the initial NKPA advance in one sector for 3
          days. However, by contrast, poor communication resulted in a large portion of the
          ROKA and their advisors getting stranded north of the Han River when the bridges
          across the river were destroyed by the South Koreans themselves on Day 4 of the
          war. The trapped KMAG advisors were forced to Escape and Evade (E&E) on foot to
          get back to Soul and link up with their higher HQ.
          Subsequently, KMAG advisors were instrumental in further delaying advancing NKPA
          forces south of the Han River and helping to organize the desperate defense of the
          Pusan perimeter. This delay provided the time and maneuver space for US forces to
          arrive. In one case, a KMAG advisor named CPT Frank Lucas single-handedly
          drafted, organized, and trained a battalion of almost 1000 South Koreans in a period
          of 5 days on his own initiative. This “regiment” then entered combat on the Pusan
          perimeter. Other KMAG advisors were instrumental in assisting the RSOI of arriving
          US forces.
          Overall, the KMAG experience highlights several key benefits and shortfalls for US
          Advisors who are deployed to an area before a surprise transition to conflict. For
          example, the lack of effective communication with US aircraft significantly impeded the
          integration of effective US Air Support, resulting in multiple incidents of fratricide. While
          the US Advisors were key to enabling the ROKA to delay the NKPA in many cases,
          this was primarily due to the ingenuity, initiative, and resolve of the individual advisors
          who acted without orders or guidance due to ineffective communication. Luckily, this
          delay was still long enough to enable US forces to get to the fight, but barely. The
          desperate RSOI in Pusan with units moving directly off the boat and into the fight was
          another area where the KMAG assistance was critical. Thus, we can see that in the
          future, all US Advisory Forces must be fully prepared to execute a variety of
          contingency plans to reduce the risk of such a near-catastrophe, even if the advisors
          are initially deploying for a “competition” advisory mission. Additionally, the selection
          of advisors who can effectively operate independently with zero guidance continues to
          be of paramount importance.

     “41 Days in Korea: From Competition to Conflict for the U.S. Military Advisory Group” by

              LTC Garrett M. Searle. Article appearing in SFA Quarterly 30th Ed., MAR 2024.

KEY CONSIDERATIONS UPON ARRIVAL IN THEATER

     3-1. Regardless of whether an advisory element is deploying into a competition, crisis, or conflict
     environment, the following key considerations must be immediately addressed:

          • Build rapport with host nation forces. Successfully establishing rapport is critical to the success of
                all advisory missions. See ATP 3-07.10 for techniques to build rapport with host nation forces.

          • Update Initial Assessment from PDSS and refine training/advisory plan as required.

46         TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                               Advisory Mission Execution

          • Coordination, collaboration, and liaison with other forces and organizations.
          • When deploying in competition, all advisors must be always prepared to immediately transition to

                crisis or directly to combat.

UPDATE INITIAL ASSESSMENT FROM PDSS

     3-2. The advisory element must update their Initial Assessment of the FSF. Things may have changed from
     the PDSS. For example, changes in leadership, FSF unit missions, or FSF unit resources may cause
     alternations to the plan. The US advisory element must be prepared for the FSF to have either greater or
     lesser capability and resources than what was assessed during the PDSS.

     3-3. If the advisory element is relieving another element in a persistent advisory mission, the advisor will
     have to make additional initial assessments during the relief in place (RIP) or transfer of authority (TOA)
     period. These cover:

          • The effectiveness of the advisor team being replaced.
          • The first and most important assessment the advisor must make is of the outgoing team to determine

                if the advisors were successful during their tour. If they have good relationships with both the FSF
                unit and the US unit, the SOPs may be valuable, and the incoming advisor can continue the
                established relationships. If the incoming team determines the outgoing team was ineffective, then
                it can begin making lists of things to fix and relationships to build. See ATP 3-07.10 for further
                information.

     3-4. If the advisory element is advising during combat operations, these will most likely be in the context of
     a multi-national coalition. Thus, additional items must now be assessed as part of the Initial Assessment:

          • The US unit operating in the AO/AI.
          • Coalition units operating in the AO/AI.

SPECIAL CONSIDERATIONS FOR UPDATING THE INITIAL
ASSESSMENT IN COMBAT

     3-5. Due to the possibility of rapidly changing situations during combat, it is possible that there may be
     significant changes between the PDSS and deployment if an advisory team is deploying to conduct combat
     advising. Thus, there are some special considerations for updating the Initial Assessment in combat. See ATP
     3-07.10 for more details.

          • Advisor team leaders should prepare their teams for an initial worst-case scenario when it comes to
                updating their initial assessment in combat.

          • Advisors will probably not have conducted predeployment training with the US units they will now
                be working with while conducting ASLA combat integration tasks.

          • US or coalition units may be unaware of the advising team’s specialized predeployment training
                and capabilities.

          • US and coalition units may not be familiar with the FSF leaders, capabilities, or limitations in the
                AO. One selling point to the partnering organization is the advising team can offer a deep
                understanding of the FSF, including personalities, culture, and human networks.

          • NGOs may have biases or restrictions against working with US military organizations like the
                advisor’s team.

          • Third-country militaries operating unilaterally may present challenges due to national caveats.
                Advisor teams should always seek guidance and the authority to coordinate directly with third-
                country militaries.

COORDINATION, COLLABORATION, AND LIAISON

     3-6. Coordination. Civilian agencies tend to operate via coordination and communication structures, rather
     than C2 structures. Although there is no command relationship between military and civilian organizations,
     clearly defined relationships may foster harmony and reduce friction.

     3-7. Collaboration. Effective operations require close coordination, cooperation, and information across
     military, interagency, and nongovernmental entities. A common technique for promoting collaboration
     between military and civilian entities is forming a civil-military operations center (CMOC) (see JP 3-57and

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  47


---

Chapter 3

     FM 3-57). Advisor units will rarely have the capability to establish a CMOC, but they should be aware if
     one is established by other US forces.

     3-8. Liaison. Direct, early liaison is a valuable source of accurate, timely information, especially where
     involvement by civilian agencies and organizations may precede military forces and present opportunities to
     enhance force effectiveness significantly. An additional benefit is an opportunity to build working
     relationships based on trust and open communications among all organizations. Ongoing liaison and an
     exchange of liaison personnel with engaged organizations are equally important.

TIPS FOR INTERACTION

     3-9. Consider these tips when interacting with various stakeholders:
          • Recognize all US Government agencies, departments, international organizations, NGOs, and
                private-sector groups in the AO.
          • If applicable, meet with other US Government agencies, NGOs, or international organization
                representatives early to understand their roles and responsibilities, policies, procedures, and goals.
                Ensure they understand the military’s mission, goals, and objectives.
          • Understand how they conduct planning and vice versa. Consider creating an integrated planning
                team or other temporary entity to address how military activities may be synchronized to
                complement their work.
          • Form a unifying goal with a desired end state. Spend time on clarifying and restating the goals to be
                achieved.
          • Support their objectives by tying short-term projects with long-term goals, without compromising
                the military mission.
          • Accept that they may not be able to allocate resources to achieve short-term results. Using resources
                of other US Government agencies is governed by law (e.g., the Economy Act), policy, and
                regulation. Advisors should involve the staff judge advocate and comptroller to ensure compliance.
                Since advisor teams do not deploy with a staff judge advocate or comptroller, this requires reach
                back to the advisor’s home station.
          • Continuously exchange information (within security restrictions) to avoid confusion over
                objectives, differences in procedures, resource limitations, and shortfalls or overlaps of authorities.
                Exchange LNOs, if acceptable to the other organization.
          • Never drop in on an international organization or NGO. Arrange meetings deliberately. Give them
                the option of meeting inside or outside military installations to maintain their appearance of
                neutrality.
          • Do not refer to NGOs or international organizations as force multipliers, partners, or any term that
                may compromise their neutrality.
          • Do not interfere with their work with the civilian population, to include elements deemed as
                unfriendly.
          • Be understanding if another organization, even within the US Government, decides not to participate
                in a project or program.
          • Respect views on bearing arms within NGO or international organization sites.
          • Use bridging agencies (e.g., the USAID Office of Civilian-Military Cooperation or United Nations
                Humanitarian Coordinator) to coordinate with other organizations (e.g., Doctors without Borders).
          • Ensure all organizations, sharing responsibility for the job, receive appropriate recognition. This
                recognition strengthens the bonds of interdependence and encourages each stakeholder to participate
                in the process by validating and reinforcing roles and responsibilities. (See ATP 3-07.10 for more
                information)

TRANSITION TO CRISIS OR ARMED CONFLICT

     3-10. SC practitioners must always be ready to immediately transition to conflict since the transition from
     competition to crisis may occur rapidly and with little to no warning. As the U.S. military footprint becomes
     more regionally aligned, it will be more important to build, maintain, leverage, and expand partner nation
     relationships and security capacity gained during competition to mitigate the lack of response time during
     crisis. Pre-positioned combat equipment to enable the rapid transition to crisis or armed conflict becomes a
     key consideration when deemed necessary.

48         TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                               Advisory Mission Execution

     3-11. While transitioning to crisis or armed conflict, it is critical for SFA forces already in the crisis or conflict
     zone to understand their legal status. All US forces are always permitted the right to self-defense. However,
     the decision to become a combatant is reserved for the highest levels of the USG. Therefore, SFA forces must
     take deliberate efforts to ensure they fully understand and abide by their combatant status as determined by
     the National Command Authority. To provide options to senior leaders, deployed SFA forces should always
     be prepared to transition to a combatant status and work with their partner security forces. SFA forces should
     also be prepared to maintain a noncombatant status, which may involve withdrawing from the country during
     a transition to crisis or conflict. If US forces withdraw from the country, it is likely that SFA forces will
     continue to support their partner forces. But they will do so from an adjacent country. In this instance, SFA
     forces will still likely work through their partner FSF to achieve theater objectives in a noncombatant role.

     3-12. Advisory elements must always have an internal plan to immediately transition directly from
     competition to conflict with no warning. Dependent on the SOFA and other agreements in place, the Advisory
     team may not immediately have access to the weapons, ammunition, and communication equipment they
     will need to effectively conduct the combat integration activities of ASLA (Advise, Support, Liaison, Assess)
     in combat. As part of their deployment planning, advisory elements should determine where they may stage
     these items to have them available if needed. In some cases, this staging may be outside the host nation
     country. In those cases where the necessary equipment is a significant distance away, advisory elements must
     also develop a plan to retrieve the equipment or have it rapidly delivered.

TIMELINE: DEPLOYMENT DAY TO D+12

D–DAY TASKS

MAIN BODY DEPLOYS

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Not applicable.
          • Remarks: Movement window planning is typically within 10 days of the deploying element’s D-

                date.

COMMERCIAL AIR PROVIDES PICKUP AND TRANSPORT OF CARGO

          • Originator: Not applicable.
          • Staffing: Not applicable.
          • Remarks: Commercial air picks up the shipment at the point of origin. Pallet riders and hazardous

                material certifiers escort commercial-air trucks to the commercial-air airport ramp station.
                Hazardous material certifiers attend commercial-air dangerous goods inspection at commercial-air
                airport ramp station. Cargo and pallet riders are transported that same night or early the following

D+1 TASKS

MAIN BODY SENDS INITIAL ENTRY REPORT

          • Originator: Deploying element.

          • Remarks: The initial entry report is sent using the standard five-line/five-W (who, what, when,

DEPLOYING ELEMENT MAKES CONTACT WITH SECURITY COOPERATION ORGANIZATION
OR EXERCISE SUPPORT GROUP TO ESTABLISH ACCOUNTABILITY IN COUNTRY

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Security cooperation organization or exercise support group.
          • Remarks: These offices are responsible for tracking the number and identity of U.S. personnel in

                country and should be made aware of your arrival so that they can provide accurate information to
                the country team and host nation. Local standard operating procedures will determine the format for
                this report, and it may be simply a verbal affirmation of the element’s arrival.

Chapter 3

D+2 THROUGH D+12 TASKS

U.S. SECURITY COOPERATION ORGANIZATION AND REGIONAL SECURITY OFFICER BRIEF
THE DEPLOYING ELEMENT

          • Originator: Security cooperation organization and regional security officer.
          • Staffing: Deploying element.
          • Remarks: The security cooperation organization and regional security officer briefs are conducted

                as required by the U.S. Embassy for all U.S. personnel in country. This brief will include specific

                organization and regional security officer should be consulted regarding plans to conduct medical
                evacuation or force protection rehearsals.

DEPLOYING ELEMENT CONDUCTS CHIEF OF MISSION BRIEF

          • Originator: Deploying element.
          • Staffing: Security cooperation office.
          • Remarks: Some ambassadors require a briefing from missions and teams rotating into county. The

                security cooperation office will schedule this briefing and provide guidance on content and format
                for the brief. Often, the format for these briefings will be informal or conversational and designed
                to assess the maturity and competence of the team. The chief of mission may request a follow-on
                briefing at the conclusion of the training event.

COMMERCIAL AIR DELIVERS CARGO

          • Originator: Commercial air.
          • Staffing: Deploying element.
          • Remarks: Commercial air and the U.S. Embassy provide import clearance, and commercial air

                delivers the cargo. Pallet riders escort the trucks, hand over original return documents to local
                commercial air team and verify return pickup schedule before departure. The deploying element
                maintains oversight of equipment status through commercial air contact.

ADVISING ELEMENT UPDATES INITIAL ASSESSMENT OF FSF, AND OUTGOING ADVISORS
AND U.S. FORCES IF APPROPRIATE

          • Originator: Deployed advisory element.
          • Staffing: Security Cooperation Organization.
          • Remarks: Upon arrival, the advisory element immediately updates its Initial Assessment that was

                developed during the PDSS. If relieving an outgoing advisory element, the incoming team also
                assesses the outgoing team’s relationship with the FSF. If arriving for combat operations, the US
                advisors also update their assessment of US and coalition forces they may be working with.

DEVELOP AND REHEARSE PLAN FOR IMMEDIATE TRANSITION TO CONFLICT

          • Originator: Deployed advisory element.
          • Staffing: Deploying element and Security Cooperation Organization
          • Remarks: Deployed advisory teams must be prepared to immediately transition to combat

                operations at all times. At a minimum, this transition plan must consist of the following elements:
                     a. Immediate force protection plan. Plan to quickly move to a secure location in case of
                           invasion/attack by 3rd party. See Appendix E, Force Protection.
                     b. Communications plan. Plan to establish communication with higher in case of EW attack.
                           Plan to establish communication with partner forces in case of transition to conflict. Plan
                           to provide US enabler support to partner force in case of transition to conflict.
                     c. Weapons and ammunition plan. Plan to retrieve/draw weapons and ammunition in case of
                           transition to conflict.
                     d. Medical support plan. Plan to obtain necessary medical support in case of transition to
                           conflict. This may have to rely on host nation support.

                     e. General classes of supply support. Plan to obtain other necessary supplies in case of

                           Agreement) with the partner nation.

DEVELOP AND REHEARSE PLAN FOR SUPPORT TO NEO (NON-COMBATANT EVACUATION
OPERATION)

          • Originator: Deployed advisory element.
          • Staffing: Security Cooperation Organization and Regional Security Officer at US Embassy.
          • Remarks: In the event of a transition to crisis or conflict, the Chief of Mission may elect to execute

                a NEO (Non-Combatant Evacuation Operation). Deployed advisory elements may find themselves
                part of the NEO in many capacities, so they should familiarize themselves with the embassy’s NEO
                plan. For example, advisors may find themselves being evacuated as non-combatants. Alternately,
                advisors may find themselves assisting the embassy staff to conduct an NEO. Or advisors may find
                themselves coordinating with host nation security forces to provide security for a NEO. In all cases,
                it is important for deployed advisory elements to fully understand the embassy NEO plan.

D+12 THROUGH MISSION COMPLETE TASKS

EXECUTION OF ADVISORY MISSION

          • Originator: Deployed advisory element.
          • Staffing: Not applicable.
          • Remarks: The advisory element executes their advisory mission IAW FM 3-22 and ATP 3-07.10.

MONITORING OF FOREIGN SECURITY FORCES DURING ADVISORY MISSION

          • Originator: Deployed advisory element.
          • Staffing: Security Cooperation Office.
          • Remarks: The advisory element continually assesses the ally/partner they are advising. Within the

                Security Cooperation paradigm of AM&E, this is known as Monitoring. The advisory element
                uploads their monitoring information to SOCIUM using approved MOPs and MOEs throughout the
                execution of their mission.
