                                                 Chapter 4

                                Assessments

    We introduced assessments in chapter 2 of this training circular. That introduction served to
    provide enough information for advisors to understand the role of the Initial Assessment and
    put that Initial Assessment in context. This chapter discusses the operation assessment process,
    highlighting the advisor’s role in monitoring, evaluating, recommending, and directing. It
    introduces the FSF Assessment Model, which includes organizational, operational,
    environmental, and institutional assessments. These assessments help advisors understand and
    estimate the capabilities and capacities of FSF. The chapter also explores long-term monitoring
    activities across multiple advisor rotations, providing insights into the evolution of partner
    forces and mission accomplishment. It emphasizes the continuous nature of the operation
    assessment process, which starts immediately upon mission receipt and continues throughout
    the mission. The chapter underscores the importance of advisors continually updating their
    assessments, as these estimates can significantly impact the progress towards achieving mutual
    US and PN objectives.

SFA ASSESSMENT, MONITORING, AND EVALUATION ACTIVITIES

     4-1. The Army supports the assessment, monitoring, and evaluating process throughout the planning and
     execution of SFA. This continuous process is used by Army forces to monitor and evaluate the performance
     and effectiveness of the U.S. and FSF, influence decision-making at appropriate levels, and initiate leader
     action to modify or cease developmental activities. This helps to determine return on investment, enabling
     policymakers to identify and enhance or eliminate ineffective initiatives, and provides reliable information
     to support policy and legislation (DODI 5132.14).

     4-2. Well-planned and executed assessment processes produce information and decisional advantages. These
     benefits include enhancing the understanding of a partner force’s capacity and capability across the entire
     governance, executive, generating, and operating construct and the operating environment.

     4-3. SFA activities alone are unlikely to achieve a strategic end state. To progress towards a strategic end
     state, multiple complementary SC activities must be conducted over time to achieve a desired result.
     Assessments are performed in close collaboration with the U.S. country team, the host-nation government,
     and other multinational partners. It is crucial for SFA planners to participate in assessment working groups
     to help determine whether SFA activities are contributing to the achievement of the strategic end state, or if
     they need to be modified or reconsidered.

FSF ASSESSMENT

     4-4. As previously discussed, advisors play a vital role in supporting the assessment, monitoring, and
     evaluation process. When assessing FSF and their supporting institutions, CCMDs, SCCs, joint task forces,
     and TSOCs use the FSF Assessment Model (Logical Order of Assessment). See Figure 4-1.

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  53


---

Chapter 4

                    Figure 4-1. FSF Assessment Model (Logical Order of Assessment)

     4-5. The FSF Assessment Model consists of four interdependent assessments—organizational, operational,
     environmental, and institutional—conducted in the order listed. Advisors can expect to support these
     assessments as well as use them to inform their planning as it relates to their specific counterparts.

     4-6. The FSF assessment is continuous, collaborative, and simultaneous in nature. While these assessments
     may seem separate and distinct, they are not—they are interdependent and reinforcing to inform decision-
     making at the appropriate levels.

ORGANIZATIONAL ASSESSMENT.

     4-7. This assessment should evaluate to what extent (scale, scope, duration parameters) an FSF core
     process/entity is organized, manned, trained, equipped, led, and resourced based on its assigned role/mission.
     To determine the organizational structure of the FSF, advisors will need to understand what the organization
     is designed to do. The organizational assessment should answer these questions: What are the FSF’s roles?
     What is the FSF mission (FSF mission analysis)? What are the FSF’s developmental goals and objectives?
     (see FM 3-22 and ATP 3-07.10).

OPERATIONAL ASSESSMENT.

     4-8. This assessment reveals strengths and weaknesses in an FSF core process/organization as it attempts to
     fulfill its directed role or purpose. This assessment follows a common operations process to determine how
     well the FSF can plan, execute, and assess their assigned missions or roles. This assessment requires that a
     core process(es)/organization demonstrate in an actual operation, exercise, or simulated environment, the
     FSF’s ability to plan, execute, and assess activities that accomplish directed missions or objectives. (see FM
     3-22 and ATP 3-07.10).

54         TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                                                   Assessments

          • A clear understanding of the FSF operational or institutional mission serves as the starting point to
                base the operational assessment. The operational assessment reveals strengths and gaps in the FSF’s
                ability to perform their missions, roles, or functions, and it measures how well the FSF can conduct
                their missions. It is not only a snapshot of FSF warfighting capabilities, but also of the FSF operating
                concept and how those functions work to support each other.

          • Assessments of FSF operational standards, capabilities, and gaps must include root cause analysis.
                For example, a unit that did not properly conduct a cordon and search may have failed because it
                lost communication with part of the cordon force, or it may have gone to the wrong location. The
                lack of communication could be because of old and worn-out equipment, poor equipment
                maintenance, insufficient training, poor leadership, lack of an SOP, or a number of other causes.
                The wrong location could be caused by incomplete intelligence, poor navigation skills, or
                navigational equipment failure. In both cases, operational assessments need to identify and address
                the root cause of why the unit did not perform to standard. (see FM 3-22 and ATP 3-07.10).

          • Operational assessments also identify FSF strengths to build on and help focus resources during
                future operations and engagements. In total, operational assessments are a critical piece of the
                advisor team handover process as discussed in appendix X.

ENVIRONMENTAL ASSESSMENT.

     4-9. This assessment considers current conditions in the environment and their effects on a specific FSF
     organization. The environmental assessment should validate the suitability of an FSF organization’s directed
     missions. This also may reveal the requirement to develop additional capabilities or capacities in the FSF
     beyond those normally associated with a similar type of security force. The environmental assessment
     answers the following question. What are the effects of PMESII on the FSF?

     4-10. Advisors working with higher headquarters staff and other SME (e.g., civil affairs) analyze each
     PMESII variable using areas, structures, capabilities, organizations, people, and events to determine the
     following:

          • To what extent a variable(s) impacts FSF capability development or the ability of the FSF to
                accomplish its mission?

          • What are the threats?
          • Are there any geographical considerations?
          • The environmental analysis must consider an understanding of current conditions that affect specific

                FSF units and their core processes. Operational variables such as PMESII positively or negatively
                influence development. For example, if a commander is perceived to be corrupt, the commander’s
                unit may be denied resources, which would hamper unit development. Conversely, units with a
                strong political or social connection may receive priority for new equipment. PMESII is only one
                of many frameworks that can be utilized to collect environmental data, so it is important to identify
                the environmental drivers or inhibitors of success in achieving mission objectives, even if they fall
                outside of a doctrinal template. (see FM 3-22 and ATP 3-07.10).
          • Environmental assessments are critical in understanding the operational environment, beginning in
                the planning process.

             Note. Advisors will need to work with their higher headquarters staff and other SMEs to conduct
             the environmental assessment.

INSTITUTIONAL ASSESSMENT

     4-11. This assessment seeks to evaluate institutional capability and capacity and determine how the FSF
     resolves gaps in one or more G-EGO functions. This assessment examines the outputs of each FSF G-EGO
     function and provides consideration for potential implications to US-provided OTEBA and funding activities.
     The institutional assessment evaluates FSF capabilities by examining the G-EGO framework holistically. An
     FSF capability is enabled by one or more legal, policy, or DOTMLPF solutions. To close existing capability
     gaps found in the institutional assessment, advisors aligned to the Service headquarters (air, land, maritime,
     cyberspace, space) equivalents of FSF will lean on DOTMLPF core process recommendations. Whereas
     advisors aligned to a ministry will lean on policy process recommendations that may impact specific

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  55


---

Chapter 4

     DOTMLPF processes or operational matters (e.g., C2, intelligence, fires, sustainment, etc.). Within DOD,
     this is collectively viewed as a DOTMLPF approach, which advisors can logically apply within and across
     FSF. However, core processes within the governance function of FSF, which establish legal authorities and
     national policy, routinely impact DOTMLPF and operational matters within FSF and its supporting
     institutions. Advisors aligned to principals and process owners within the executive function of FSF will
     often collaborate with US interagency advisors to inform the development of a PN’s parliament or national
     security-like council. The institutional assessment will typically be formally conducted and maintained at the
     CCMD, SCC, or TSOC level, but advisors will likely be asked to monitor (observe and analyze) information
     and make recommendations pertinent to the FSF as an institution using the elements of each G-EGO function.
     (see FM 3-22 and ATP 3-07.10).

FSF MONITORING

     4-12. Monitoring is a continuous observation of conditions relevant to the current operation. It involves not
     only joint forces carrying out activities but also expects advisors, in concert with their counterparts, to
     monitor the activities of the Friendly Security Forces (FSF) to inform decision-making at the appropriate
     levels. (see FM 3-22 and ATP 3-07.10).

     4-13. This process speaks to the long stare (over multiple advisor rotations) at how partner forces and mission
     accomplishment are evolving. It enables planners and advisors to understand changes in the operational
     environment based on advising activities. This understanding further informs a commander’s intent, guidance
     for planning, prioritization, and execution.

     4-14. Monitoring involves collecting and analyzing relevant indicators of performance or effectiveness and
     conditions in an operational environment that affect those indicators. The FSF Assessment Model provides
     a tool for advisors to monitor the activities of the FSF, focusing on tasks carried out by FSF, normally using
     Measures of Performance (MOPs) and Measures of Effectiveness (MOEs). This monitoring informs and is
     informed by assessments at the strategic, operational, and tactical levels within the joint force and the FSF.

     4-15. Within the assessment process, monitoring allows staffs to collect relevant information, specifically
     that information about the current situation that can be compared to the forecasted situation described in the
     commander’s intent and concept of operations. Progress cannot be judged, nor effective decisions made,
     without an accurate understanding of the current situation, including gender and sociocultural information.
     Monitoring, therefore, enables evaluation and measurement of developmental activity progress toward a
     desired end state.

FSF EVALUATION

     4-16. Evaluation is a critical process that involves using specific criteria to judge progress towards desired
     conditions and determining the reasons behind the current degree of progress. This process is at the heart of
     the assessment process, where most of the analysis occurs. It is primarily a commander’s responsibility but
     can be conducted at any level.

     4-17. The evaluation process involves the systematic collection and analysis of information and evidence
     about the characteristics and outcomes of an ongoing or completed activity. This could be an SC activity
     such as advising, its design, implementation, and results. The process helps SFA planners and practitioners
     determine what is working and what is not, providing them with insights into how to accomplish the mission.

     4-18. Evaluations play a crucial role in determining an advisor mission’s relevance, efficiency, sustainability,
     and impact with respect to mission accomplishment. This information forms the basis for improving
     effectiveness and informing decision-makers regarding future plans, programs, and activities.

     4-19. Distinct from initial or follow-on assessment and monitoring, evaluation focuses on documenting the
     achievement of objectives, the attainment of end states, and in some cases, the value of continuing advising
     activities. Evaluations at the strategic level depend on operation evaluations, which are critical in explaining
     why certain efforts succeed or fail.

     4-20. Evaluations are typically administered by teams from a CCMD, SCC, or TSOC headquarters. However,
     advisors also play a significant role in providing objective assessments and collecting focused data/feedback

56  TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                                                   Assessments

     on behalf of the evaluation team. At the tactical and operational level, advisors normally contribute to the
     evaluation process through assessments.

     4-21. Continual assessments depict changes in the operational environment and track progress, effects, and
     end state or objective achievement status. This makes them a primary tool for evaluation teams. All key
     participants in a particular activity or operation should be committed to seeing the mission objectively. They
     should also be involved in the development of the operation assessment process or monitoring plan, collection
     of relevant data, and reporting. Evaluation is a comprehensive process that involves various levels of
     command and requires the active participation of all key players. It is a vital tool for understanding the
     effectiveness of operations and making informed decisions for future planning and activities. (see FM 3-22
     and ATP 3-07.10).

SFA ASSESSMENT, MONITORING AND EVALUATION
CONSIDERATIONS

     4-22. Assessment Interaction During the planning phase, it’s crucial for SFA planners and practitioners to
     collaborate to assign appropriate MOEs and MOPs to developmental activities. These indicators should
     ideally mirror the intended outcomes of the activities, thereby facilitating the attainment of broader strategic
     goals and end states. Figure 4-2 on page 63 offers a helpful illustration of the assessment interaction
     framework. (see FM 3-22 and ATP 3-07.10).

                                     Figure 4-2. Assessment interaction

4-23. SFA AM&E Considerations When conducting AM&E of a partner FSF, it’s important to consider
cultural aspects. SFA planners and practitioners must be cognizant of the cultural differences between USG
standards and the host nation’s norms when assessing the effectiveness of a partner security force. Ignoring
these cultural differences could hinder progress and distort results. What theater army planners might deem
as inadequate capability and capacity standards could be perfectly acceptable to the partner force.

4-24. Striking the right balance between qualitative and quantitative measurements of the partner FSF within
the CCP and country plans is vital. AM&E tend to be more qualitative at the strategic and operational levels,
with the availability of hard data potentially influencing the balance of quantitative assessments.

4-25. AM&E of a partner nation force’s “will to fight” is particularly important in crisis and conflict
situations. Factors such as military leadership, morale, cohesion, discipline, culture, and history of the partner
nation are crucial in assessing the will to fight. Understanding these factors is key to evaluating combat

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  57


---

     effectiveness as it can directly impact U.S. national and theater strategy and future resource commitments.

     4-26. SFA planners and practitioners should also consider the “lag time” in the AM&E process, the
     methodology, and the cause-effect relationship between an effort or set of efforts and the achieved results.
     It’s important not to prematurely draw conclusions on causal linkages and to have realistic expectations for
     progress in terms of when outcomes and end states will be realized.

     4-27. AM&E not only guides activity development by theater army SFA planners but also inform decision-
     making by the combatant command and country team. Commanders must endorse the methodology. To
     ensure success, SFA planners within the combatant commands and theater armies need to maintain flexibility,
     provide proper AM&E training across all staff elements, and invest significant time and effort in AM&E
     resources. (see FM 3-22 and ATP 3-07.10).

                all open contracts, determine which need to be descoped or terminated and which remain mission-
                essential or needed for life, health, and safety until departure from the operational area. When
                contractors have satisfactorily completed performance of contracts and final payments have been
                made, contract files should be closed out as soon as possible. Some typical contract closeout actions

                 Redeploying element validates that contracted service is no longer needed and notifies
                     supporting contracting activity to descope or terminate.
                 Contracting officer representative (if appointed) submits final invoice to the contracting officer,
                     ensures all contractor equipment is removed and all government furnished property (if provided
                     to a contractor) is returned, and submits completed contracting officer representative file to
                     contracting officer for proper file storage. Any funds not expended are de-obligated.

                                                Appendix A

                  Advisor Screening and Selection

    In the dynamic and complex landscape of security cooperation, the role of advisors is pivotal
    in fostering effective partnerships and enhancing the capabilities of allied and partner nations.
    The selection and deployment of the right advisors can significantly influence the success of
    security cooperation initiatives, ensuring that partner nations receive the expertise and support
    they need to address their security challenges. This chapter delves into the critical process of
    advisor screening and selection, highlighting the importance of identifying individuals with the
    right mix of technical skills, cultural sensitivity, and strategic vision. By exploring best
    practices, key considerations, and the essential criteria for selecting advisors, this chapter aims
    to provide a comprehensive guide for ensuring that security cooperation efforts are led by
    competent, well-suited professionals who can drive meaningful and sustainable outcomes.

ADVISOR SELECTION

     A-1. Advisors are specially selected and trained personnel who understand the operational environment and
     provide effective leadership. Providing mentorship, ensuring unity of effort and purpose, and establishing
     sustainability help build legitimacy for operations by the FSF and governance by the HNs.

     A-2. The success of the advisor mainly depends on personal attributes centered on human relations. The
     successful advisor is one who can analyze the situation and that of the FSF and HN colleagues and to adjust
     the counterpart’s decisions and behavior accordingly. In addition to the technical and tactical competence
     necessary to validate an advisor’s capacity, advising requires having other attributes that cultivate
     relationships, such as empathy, patience, and the ability to influence without manipulating. Not every Soldier
     is well suited to perform advisory functions. Although certain individuals seem to instinctively possess the
     requisite skill set, others must undergo extensive interpersonal training. The selection and training process
     for advisors is designed to optimize the inherent abilities of the Soldier through a variety of training and
     operational experiences. Because advisors operate in very ambiguous environments, it is difficult to establish
     objective criteria to assess potential advisors. However, research and experience indicate that several
     personality traits greatly enhance the Soldier’s ability to adapt and thrive in a foreign culture. Of the traits
     listed below, no single trait is paramount. Advisor selection and training programs seek to develop an
     understanding of the contributions for each trait. These traits include—

          • Tolerance for ambiguity.
          • Situational awareness.
          • Realistic goal and task setting.
          • Ability to accept and learn from failure.
          • Open-mindedness.
          • Emotional and social intelligence.
          • Ability to withhold judgment.
          • Maturity.
          • Empathy.
          • Adaptability.
          • Ability to communicate effectively.
          • Tactfulness.
          • Flexibility.
          • Diplomatic conduct.
          • Intellectual curiosity.
          • Collaborative nature.
          • Humility.
          • Persistence.
          • Motivation of self and others.
          • Leadership.
          • Self-reliance.

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  67


---

Appendix A

          • Integrity.
          • Strong sense of self.
          • Language capability.
          • Tolerance for differences.

Cross-Cultural Communication

     A-3. Communication is the transfer of messages from one person to the next. These messages may be passed
     along verbally, in writing, or by signals. In essence, the sender encodes the message, and the receiver decodes
     it. The type and style of encoding used is based upon the sender’s history, beliefs, values, prejudices, attitudes,
     and preferences. The receiver decodes messages based upon lifestyle, group membership and rank,
     worldview, status, language, and social practice. Communication is a two-way process in which the encoding
     and decoding methods can affect both sending and receiving. Effective communication occurs when the
     message is perceived and responded to in the manner that the sender intended. In effective communication
     primarily occurs from poorly chosen words, flawed timing, a confused mixture of verbal and nonverbal
     signals, poor listening skills, and communication noise. Communication noise describes the influences that
     detract from effective communication.

CULTURAL GAP BARRIERS

     A-4. Each culture has its own rules regarding who a person may speak to, how and when the person may
     speak, and what topics the person may speak about. Many cultures rely heavily on nonverbal signals to
     communicate. In such cultures, posture, expression, and actions convey more than spoken or written words.
     Language is the ultimate communication barrier. The successful advisor should study, at a minimum,
     common phrases in the HN’s language. In addition, advisors must recognize that there are topics of
     conversation that should be avoided whenever possible (for example, religion, politics, ideology, and
     personal questions).

Developing a Sense of Cultural Awareness

     A-5. Advisors must recognize they are a product of their own culture. They must learn as much as possible
     about the culture of the people with whom they need to communicate. When communicating with people
     across cultures, advisors must abandon any sentiments of ethnocentrism—the tendency of individuals to
     judge all other groups according to their own group’s standards, behaviors, and customs. Such notions lead
     an individual to see other groups as inferior by comparison.

Recognizing Differences

     A-6. Each culture has its own way of accomplishing required daily tasks. Advisors must understand that
     each society may approach things differently; it does not mean that the society is inefficient or less intelligent.
     Being different should not be viewed as a negative. Respect for counterparts must be always maintained.

Learning to Adapt

     A-7. Advisors must be flexible and ready to adapt and adjust their behavior; however, they must be careful
     not to overdo their flexibility. Individuals who are overly flexible are often perceived as being insincere. The
     successful advisor must strive to act in a way that is appropriate to the target culture. Above all, advisors
     should be themselves and show sincerity.

Developing Tolerance

     A-8. Advisors must develop a tolerance for deviations from accepted norms. Events or activities that may
     seem extraordinary to newcomers may be common practice in the culture. Advisors must be aware that
     members of the foreign culture may be astounded by that which is considered commonplace in the United
     States. Careful observation should be made before judgments are rendered about seemingly peculiar
     behavior.

68  TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                        Advisor Screening and Selection

Interpreting the Clash of Cultural Values

     A-9. Cultural incongruity between the advisor and the environment can lead to varying levels of
     misinterpretation and friction. Values that one holds can be particularly sensitive for both the advisors and
     the people they deal with. A clash in values can lead the advisor to regard those of another culture as
     unwelcome, unnatural, backwards, or immoral. Advisors must be careful to avoid reactions that subvert their
     own purposes. For example, a graft functions similarly to a show of allegiance to a superior in certain non-
     Western cultures. Bribery, being associated with corruption in the West, is looked upon as criminal, whereas
     in many non-Western countries, bribery is part of social existence (largely based on political or fiscal
     survival). Most Americans would view a bribe with moral indignation. An advisor may anger many non-
     Westerners if reacting to a patronage system with moral outrage. Advisors must carefully weigh the costs
     and benefits to the mission, as well as their approach in dealing with such situations and look for a palatable
     solution for all parties. As in the case of a patronage system, it is best not to reinforce it by refraining from
     doing what the counterpart asks if the request only serves to reinforce it. The advisor should deliberately
     explain why such requests cannot be fulfilled and the adverse effects on the decision-making process, the
     HN unit, and the HN. Furthermore, an advisor should ensure the counterpart understands that the
     recommendation is not personal but instead is based on principle to maintain rapport.

Personal Contact

     A-10. Personal contact is the most effective way to bridge organizational barriers. Interorganizational
     stumbling blocks are very real and the prejudices that arise from them are exacerbated by misunderstanding
     and ignorance. Often, it is too easy to attribute negative attitudes and hostile motives to faceless groups.
     Advisors must employ superior interpersonal skills and deal directly and closely with individual members of
     other organizations. This type of contact can effectively reinforce commonality and diminish the impact of
     disparity. These personal relationships are the key to effective interorganizational relationships.

FACTORS AFFECTING COMMUNICATION ACROSS CULTURES

     A-11. Five key aspects greatly impact communication across cultures. These factors are:
          • Level of formality.
          • Level of directness and explicitness.
          • Perception of time.
          • Perception of the individual versus the group.
          • Demonstration of emotion.

LEVEL OF FORMALITY

     A-12. Levels of formality vary greatly from culture to culture. Most Asian cultures are on the high end of the
     formality continuum. In contrast, the North American culture places very little emphasis on formality.
     Advisors must recognize the importance placed on formality and adjust their behavior accordingly. To most
     Germans, chewing gum when receiving a briefing may indicate that the individual is not paying attention.
     This translates into lack of respect for the speaker and may even be construed as offensive. Conversely,
     Americans can be quite casual at work. Chewing gum during a presentation may not indicate a lack of
     attention. In certain settings, it may not even be considered a lack of manners. To be successful, advisors
     must be aware of such cultural inconsistencies. A simple action that is completely acceptable in one culture
     may be considered distasteful in another. Time spent researching such issues of formality serves as an
     advantage when serving in an advisory capacity.

LEVEL OF DIRECTNESS AND EXPLICITNESS

     A-13. Certain cultures are very direct and explicit in their communication, whereas other cultures are indirect
     and vague. Most people from Asian and Middle Eastern cultures place a high reliance on shared experience,
     nonverbal cues, and the context in which the communication takes place. Consequently, they often seem
     indirect and vague in their verbal communication. In some countries, such as the United States, Switzerland,
     and Germany, people tend to rely heavily on the spoken word for communication. Consequently, verbal
     interactions are very direct, precise, and explicit. Reliance on context is low, as is reliance on nonverbal cues.
     To outsiders, this style of communication may be perceived as too direct and overly talkative. The

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  69


---

Appendix A

     indirectness that characterizes communication in some cultures is often a strategy designed to prevent another
     person from losing face. It may be viewed as consideration for another person’s sense of dignity. In cultures
     that are direct and explicit in their communication, however, this indirectness may be perceived as dishonesty
     or insincerity, suggesting that the speaker may have something to hide.

     A-14. When communicating with people from a more indirect culture, it is important to exercise extra
     caution—both in what is said and how it is said. Being overly direct may result in unintentional offense.
     Careful attention must also be given to nonverbal cues, shared experiences, and the circumstances in which
     the communication takes place. The true meanings of messages may reside more in these nonverbal cues
     than in the words uttered. When communicating with people from a more direct culture, typical American
     candor is more appropriate. When dealing with more direct cultures, ill-prepared advisors may become
     offended when ideas or opinions are attacked with an unaccustomed degree of directness. To be successful,
     advisors must bear in mind that some cultures employ such directness as a technique to achieve clarity.
     Advisors must be objective in listening to what their counterparts have to say and remember that attacks on
     ideas are not intended to be personal or deliberately embarrassing. Finally, because direct cultures place little
     reliance on context, advisors must pay particular attention to the spoken word.

PERCEPTION OF TIME

     A-15. Cultures may differ greatly in their perception of time. Edward Hall, a prominent researcher in the
     field of intercultural communication, made a useful distinction between monochronic-time and polychronic-
     time cultures:

          • In monochronic-time cultures (most Western countries), members place a great emphasis on
                Schedules, precise reckoning of time, and promptness. In such cultures, the schedule takes
                precedence over the interpersonal relation. Furthermore, because of this urgency to maintain
                schedules, members of such cultures tend to get to the point quickly. This directness may be viewed
                as rude or brash in polychronic-time cultures.

          • In polychronic-time cultures, time is viewed as fluid. Members of polychronic-time societies do not
                observe strict schedules—agendas are subordinate to interpersonal relations. Most Asian countries
                (as well as several Latin American and Middle Eastern countries) are considered polychronic-time

          • cultures.

     A-16. By knowing how the concept of time is embraced, advisors can better adapt to a new cultural
     environment. Because the United States is a monochronic-time culture, Americans must learn to be patient
     with polychronic-time cultures for whom punctuality is, at best unimportant, and at worst a negative trait.

Perception of the Individual Versus the Group

     A-17. Cultures may be individualistic or collective in their orientation. Geert Hofstede was another prominent
     researcher in intercultural communication who describes an individualist culture as one in which the ties
     between individuals are loose—where people are expected to take care of themselves and their immediate
     families. In a collectivist culture, people are raised from birth into strong, cohesive groups. These groups
     offer a lifetime of protection in exchange for unquestioning loyalty. In individualist cultures, the individual
     takes center stage. Independence is highly valued. Individuals earn credit or blame for the success or failure
     of their endeavors. In collectivist cultures, an individual is regarded as a part of the group. Interdependence
     prevails among individuals in the same group. Credit or blame for success or failure belongs to the entire
     group. Individuals do not seek recognition and often are uncomfortable if it is offered.

Show of Emotion

     A-18. Each culture has its own system of expressing emotion. Some cultures tend to be very expressive with
     their emotions and show their feelings plainly by laughing, grimacing, or scowling. Other cultures tend to be
     more repressive. Rather than showing their feelings openly, members of these cultures keep their emotions
     controlled and subdued. When people from these two cultures interact, misunderstandings are common.
     Those from the more expressive culture may view people from the repressive culture as cold or unfeeling.
     Similarly, those from the more repressive culture may view their more expressive colleagues as immature
     and eccentric. Advisors must avoid snap judgments. To communicate effectively, the advisor must be able
     to display the appropriate level of emotion.

70  TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                        Advisor Screening and Selection

Nonverbal Communication

     A-19. Nonverbal communication uses facial expressions, gestures, physical contact, and body postures to
     convey meaning. Although there are many excellent books on the subject, virtually every publication
     discusses nonverbal communication from a particular cultural vantage point. Nonverbal communication and
     culture are inseparable; one can only properly understand this type of communication if one understands the
     culture from which it originates.

Body Language

     A-20. Body language and gestures are products of custom, and they are just as important to communication
     as spoken or written language. The improper use of a gesture—even as simple as a thumbs-up gesture or a
     two-finger peace sign—can upset a carefully nurtured relationship. The most basic survival gesture—the
     smile—is a visual signal that is universally understood. However, even though the smile is understood in
     every culture, some fine distinctions do exist between cultures. Russians, for example, very rarely smile on
     the street. The French believe Americans smile too much. Japanese people seldom smile during (what they
     consider to be) formal circumstances, such as a photograph for a driver’s license or Christmas card.
     Malaysians and Indonesians tend to smile—even giggle—when they feel nervous or embarrassed.

     A-21. The common symbol for OK, made by touching the tips of thumb and index finger, is one of the best-
     known gestures in the United States. In other cultures, however, the same gesture may mean something
     entirely different. For example—

          • In the south of France, this gesture is used to indicate zero or something worthless.
          • An individual who uses the symbol to indicate OK may negate a negotiated arrangement or offend

                a counterpart by indicating that the deal (or the individual) is worthless.
          • In Japan, this gesture is used to indicate a coin or money. An individual who uses the symbol to

                indicate OK may offend the counterpart by appearing to ask for a bribe.
          • In Brazil, Germany, and Russia, this gesture is used to indicate intimate body parts.
          • An advisor carelessly using this gesture may appear to be suggesting something unintended.

     A-22. Advisors must pay particular attention to seemingly minor differences. For example, Americans tend
     to beckon a waiter by raising a single finger. That type of pointing gesture is frowned upon in Japan, where
     pointing is done with the thumb of a closed fist. In most of Europe, a person beckons a waiter holding their
     hand in front of them (fingertips forward with the palm facing the floor) and making a scratching motion
     with the fingers. Another common beckoning gesture in the United States is made by holding the index finger
     straight up (facing inward) and curling it up and down. In Australia, Indonesia, and Mexico, this gesture is
     reserved for animals and prostitutes. The use of body language in the AO should be studied thoroughly before
     deployment. Once deployed, advisors should continue to study the body language used by local nationals.
     The surrounding circumstances must also be considered (for example, how the people call a waiter or how
     they wave goodbye). Advisors should observe posture, body language, and common gestures, such as people
     tapping the sides of their noses, or flipping the lobes of their ears.

Interpersonal Distance

     A-23. The space people maintain around their bodies reflects the desire to control the people who can get
     close to them and the circumstances in which it is deemed acceptable. An individual who violates another’s
     interpersonal space without consent is usually perceived as hostile or aggressive. The perception of
     appropriate interpersonal distance varies between cultures and reflects the style and tone of the society. Figure
     A-1 on page 78, provides an intentionally broad overview of these variations. However, the guidelines
     provided cannot be applied to every relationship or circumstance, even within a single cultural group. Few
     areas of nonverbal communication are more sensitive than interpersonal space. Fortunately, the appropriate
     distance is easy to respect once it is identified. For Soldiers serving in advisory roles, interpersonal distance
     is particularly important when administering or receiving criticism. In such instances, individuals must be
     sure they do not stand too close to one another. An interpersonal gap that is too short can make one, or both
     individuals feel threatened and defensive. People who feel defensive have trouble expressing themselves
     clearly, and they may become resistant to suggestion, correction, and criticism and may even become
     aggressive.

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  71


---

Appendix A

                                           Figure A-1. Interpersonal Distance

Touch

     A-24. Touch should be minimized when communicating across cultural lines. Although some cultures are
     more open to touching than others, even the most demonstrative groups have rules of propriety and etiquette.
     Physical contact made at the wrong time can cause serious misunderstandings. In mainstream American
     culture, touching is generally discouraged. Native-born Americans tend to abandon touch at an early age and
     substitute words as the primary means of communication. Northern Europeans, such as people from
     Germany, Scandinavia, and the United Kingdom, are similarly uncomfortable with touch from anyone other
     than intimate family members or friends.

     A-25. Touching is discouraged in most Asian cultures, particularly to the back, head, or shoulder. In addition,
     small children are not to be touched by anyone outside the immediate family. Whereas it is common for an
     American adult to playfully tousle the hair of a small child, such actions may be deemed extremely offensive
     in some Asian cultures. In the example of the small child’s hair, an advisor’s well-meant attempt to ease
     tension, display a sense of humor, and build trust would achieve the exact opposite. There are rules for
     touching.

     A-26. Because U.S. society is so aware of the potential for people to use negative touch to intimidate or
     threaten, most Americans are careful in how they touch. Researchers classify Americans as low touchers in
     relation to the rest of the world. However, the use of touch in such a multicultural society varies. Some
     Americans never touch anyone outside their immediate family, even though they may prize such a person’s
     friendship. Others touch often—usually on the shoulders and arms—but such contact is not designed to
     express any real meaning. In the United States, touch is used mainly when individuals meet or depart (for
     example, a handshake or a casual hug).

     A-27. Americans can simulate the feeling of touch (without ever touching) by allowing others to move in
     close when talking. Close friends may exchange hugs, pretend punches, or kisses and touch frequently when
     talking. For acquaintances and superiors, a simple handshake is all that is expected. Some people are
     considered high touchers and give friendly arm, back, and shoulder touches—even to new acquaintances.
     Some Americans show in public what might be considered private expressions of affection in other cultures
     (for example, prolonged kisses or other overt displays of affection).

72          TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                        Advisor Screening and Selection

     A-28. Handshakes exchanged in the United States tend to be more firm than other cultures. For instance,
     Chinese, Japanese, and most Africans use light handshakes. This does not indicate that they are unassertive;
     it simply is a cultural trait. Russians and most Europeans use firm handshakes. Similarly, this does not
     necessarily imply that they are assertive or tough.

Eye Contact

     A-29. Rules for appropriate eye contact vary among cultures; however, few people consciously recognize
     that such rules even exist. Still, individuals usually act according to society norms. These individuals have
     unconsciously absorbed the rules of their culture. Among the majority of North Americans, the accepted rule
     is for individuals to make intermittent eye contact with the people to whom they are speaking. If people hold
     eye contact for too long, they may be considered aggressive. If people hold eye contact too briefly, they may
     come across as disinterested. An individual who consistently fails to make eye contact may appear evasive
     or Dishonest. Advisors must be sensitive to cultural variations when making eye contact. In some African
     cultures, for example, it is considered impolite to make more than the briefest eye contact. To most
     Americans, such an exchange would imply that the other party was distracted and not paying attention.

Facial Expressions and Gestures

     A-30. It is not unusual for Americans to use facial expressions to convey doubt, surprise, distrust, anger,
     agreement, or rejection. Other cultures are less expressive with facial expressions. For example, a Japanese
     counterpart’s face may be expressionless, but inside, that partner may be furious. Similarly, the partner may
     enthusiastically agree with a recommendation, although their outward expression might be interpreted as
     indifference. Most Americans use many hand gestures. In most European and Pacific Rim countries, hand
     gestures are kept to a minimum. In Latin America, the Middle East, and Africa, it is also common for people
     to use hand gestures to emphasize their points of view. Advisors must exercise caution in both the number
     and magnitude of hand gestures.

Communication with Illiterates

     A-31. Soldiers frequently operate in developing countries—and even some industrialized nations—where
     large segments of the population are either illiterate or semiliterate. In many cases (such as in Haiti), this
     segment of the population represents the primary target audience with whom the unit must communicate. An
     illiterate or semiliterate audience presents a challenge that requires creative and enhanced communication
     skills. To reach an illiterate or semiliterate segment of the population, there are several existing channels of
     communication that can be used; others can be created. Radio is a powerful means of reaching illiterate or
     semiliterate populations. Other techniques include interpersonal communication, traditional or folk
     communication, simple graphics, and other basic printed materials. When dealing with an illiterate or
     semiliterate audience, the advisor should:

          • Determine how people receive information. The lack of literacy skills does not necessarily
                indicate an absence in the flow of information.

          • Identify the key communicators for the target audience. Very often, word of mouth is the best.
                way to share information among audiences for whom written materials are ineffective.

          • Identify the informal communications networks (the grapevine) in the community. In some areas,
                the most effective communication networks lack leadership or formal structure. Advisors must
                identify members of the community who trust the unit and are willing to help.

          • Include the use of printed materials. Photo novels, comic books, and wall posters that use more
                graphics and fewer words can convey powerful messages.

          • Cater visual communication materials to the target audience. Images that effectively convey a
                message in one culture might carry an entirely different meaning in another. Artwork should be
                tested on a sample audience.

TOPICS TO APPROACH WITH SPECIAL CAUTION

     A-32. Each culture has topics that are considered forbidden. These subjects are simply not discussed in casual
     conversation. The nature of these topics can vary widely; however, advisors should avoid (at least initially)
     the following four subjects:

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  73


---

Appendix A

          • Religion
          • Politics
          • Personal matters
          • Fitness

Religion

     A-33. Discussing religion can immediately destroy the communication process, particularly if the local
     nationals believe that offense was given. Whether actual or perceived, the damage done may be very
     profound, as cultural sensitivities may be acute. This is evident in the case of Islam, for example.

Politics

     A-34. Political discussions can become very heated and should be avoided if possible. In regions where
     political issues dominate the news, avoiding such discussions may prove difficult. An advisor must ensure
     that these types of discussions are handled in a diplomatic manner if there is no polite way to avoid it.

Personal Matters

     A-35. It is considered rude to ask other Americans highly personal questions. Advisors should extend the
     same courtesy to members of the local national population. In certain rural areas of West Africa, where the
     world is seen in generalities, it is considered extremely rude to ask specific questions.

Fitness

     A-36. Physical skills and the resulting personal fitness can be critical to personal and professional credibility
     when dealing with military counterparts. Fatigue reduces overall capability, diminishes perceptiveness,
     impairs thought processes, and impedes cross-cultural communication. Physical training programs are readily
     adaptable to the requirements of a given target culture and region.

REGIONAL-SPECIFIC PROFICIENCY

     A-37. As knowledge of a given culture is developed, certain activities or capabilities may emerge as critical
     to cross-cultural communications and effective counterpart relations. For example, some level of proficiency
     in a popular local sport may help establish interpersonal relationships that permit more effective
     communication and greater influence. Soccer is an example of a widely played sport. More localized
     examples include horseback or camel riding; swimming; trapping; or even singing, dancing, and playing
     musical instruments. Small investments in skills seemingly unrelated to military mission requirements can
     provide disproportionately high returns in influence.

FINAL TIPS ON COMMUNICATION

     A-38. Researching a target audience’s culture can be an endless activity. Advisors must not become bogged
     down in the minute details of a single cultural feature. Instead, they should strive to understand all aspects of
     the culture. If advisors demonstrate a basic knowledge and a willingness to listen and learn, they will achieve
     a more effective exchange. Advisors should consult with individuals who live in the HN and know the local
     customs. A one-hour interview with a native may be more productive than a week of formal language and
     culture Training.

     A-39. Effective communication requires practice. Advisors should rehearse to practice favorable body
     language and to eliminate unfavorable gestures and postures. Body language is as important as the verbal
     message. It should appear natural—not labored or uncomfortable. Advisors must recognize that an American
     stereotype—possibly a negative stereotype—likely precedes them. They should try to understand and discern
     this stereotype. Over time, advisors may be able to improve the local impression of Americans.

ADVISOR TECHNIQUES

     A-40. The most important mission of an advisor is to enhance the military professionalism of the HN
     counterpart. An advisor must influence the HN military and prepare them to deal with the changing

74  TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                        Advisor Screening and Selection

     environment by emphasizing civilian control and demonstrating the advantages of a democratic system of
     government.

     A-41. A major cause of failure by an advisor is the inability to maintain a good working relationship with the
     counterpart. The unsuccessful advisor often fails to understand why the counterparts do not feel the same
     sense of urgency. On occasion, an advisor is unable to realize that the counterpart will remain and continue
     to fight (or prepare to fight) the adversary long after the advisor’s end of tour and to the safety of the U.S.

     A-42. The advisor must know HN sociopolitical and military organizations and their interrelationships— to
     include personalities, political movements, forces involved, and social drives. With this knowledge the
     advisor must encourage the counterpart to understand the need for an integrated civil-military effort the
     counterpart must learn that military actions are subordinate to, and supportive of, the economic and social
     actions required to defeat the adversary.

     A-43. Rapport is a symbiotic relationship between people based on mutual trust, understanding, and respect.
     Personal dislike, animosity, and other forms of friction characterize the lack of rapport. The need to establish
     rapport with the HN counterpart is the result of a unique military position in which the advisor has no direct
     authority or control over the actions of the HN partner. However, an advisor can influence or motivate the
     counterpart to act in certain ways by using the proper advisory techniques.

     A-44. Rapport results when everyone perceives the other as competent, mature, responsible, and compatible
     (working toward a common goal). If the advisor can convey this attitude to the HN counterpart, long-lasting
     and effective rapport will exist.

ADVISOR CONSIDERATIONS

     A-45. Advising the counterpart to select a particular course of action (COA) is only effective if the
     counterpart perceives that the advisor is professionally competent to provide sound advice. If the counterpart
     does not perceive the proposed solution to a problem as realistic, the competence of the advisor may be
     questioned. The advisor must make every effort to explain to the counterpart that the recommended COA is
     realistic and effective.

     A-46. Advisors must be flexible and ready to adjust their behavior; however, they must be careful not to
     overdo their flexibility. Individuals who are overly flexible are often perceived as being insincere. The
     successful advisor must strive to act in a way that is appropriate to the target culture. Above all, an advisor
     should be genuine and show sincerity. Over compartmentalizing must be avoided, as distrust can be prevented
     simply by not being overly secretive.

     A-47. The prospect that an advisor may—from time to time share in the same risks of HN counterparts to
     properly set the example should be clear. This shared risk may especially be critical in a combat environment.
     By sharing the same risks as that of the HN counterparts, the advisor will dispel any appearance of a
     privileged status that may potentially harm relations or lose rapport.

     A-48. Awareness of cultural differences and any impact is the first prerequisite for successfully working with
     a counterpart. Being sensitive to the fact that differences exist and carefully observing actions and reactions
     can enable the Soldier to adjust behavior and modify actions to achieve greater influence with the counterpart.

     A-49. Advisors must recognize they are a product of their own culture. They must learn as much as possible
     about the culture of the people with whom they are expected to communicate with. Advisors must understand
     that each society may approach things differently; it does not mean that the society is inefficient or less
     intelligent. Being different should not be viewed as negative. Respect for counterparts must always be
     maintained. Advisors must avoid snap judgments. To communicate effectively, the advisor must be able to
     display the appropriate level of emotion.

     A-50. Advisors must develop a tolerance for deviations from accepted norms. Events or activities that may
     seem extraordinary to newcomers may be common practice in the culture. Advisors must be aware that
     members of the foreign culture may be astounded by that which is considered commonplace in the United
     States. Careful observation should be made before judgments are rendered about seemingly peculiar
     behavior.

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  75


---

Appendix A

     A-51. Advisors often must work with counterparts with different or opposing values. Any time spent trying
     to change the basic values of the counterpart is time wasted; furthermore, such efforts are likely to create
     lasting resentment. Advisors should have some understanding of the value system of their counterpart. Values
     are usually nonnegotiable; they are basic rules about what is right and wrong.

     A-52. Advisors must be careful to avoid reactions that subvert their own purposes. A clash in values can lead
     the advisor to regard those of another culture as unwelcome, unnatural, backwards, or immoral. In dealing
     with a counterpart who has conflicting values, it is typically best to persuade the individual that the desired
     action or behavior is not really in conflict with the counterpart’s values. If an action is contrary to the advisor’s
     values, the advisor should explain the adverse effects on the decision-making process, the HN unit, and the
     HN. Furthermore, an advisor should ensure his counterpart understands that any recommendation is not
     personal, but instead is based on principle to maintain rapport.

     A-53. Advisor should strive to establish and maintain good rapport by conveying to the counterpart sincere
     interest in the counterpart, the nation of the counterpart, and its cause; the advisor will not belittle the
     counterpart or effort made by the counterpart and must ensure the counterpart understands that the advisor
     has no intention of taking over. Advisors must demonstrate that they have come to help because they believe
     the counterparts’ goals to be just, fair, and deserving of success. Genuine rapport is slow to develop and can
     be ruined in an instant.

     A-54. When advising in a combat environment, decentralized elements often rely on indigenous forces for
     security and for establishing, building, and maintaining rapport with all foreign nationals. Rapport is a critical
     part of the force protection plan as it is meant to keep the peace with counterparts and potentially provide
     early warnings of an imminent outbreak of hostilities or instances of electromagnetic warfare.

     A-55. Advisors must recognize the importance placed on formality and adjust behaviors accordingly. A
     simple action that is completely acceptable in one culture may be considered distasteful in another. Time
     spent researching such issues of formality serves as an advantage when serving in an advisory capacity.

     A-56. Advisors must not become bogged down in the minute details of a single cultural feature. Instead, they
     should strive to understand all aspects of the culture. If advisors demonstrate a basic knowledge and a
     willingness to listen and learn, they will achieve a more effective exchange. Advisors should consult with
     individuals who live in the HN and know the local customs. A one-hour interview with a native may be more
     productive than a week of formal language and culture training.

     A-57. Advisors should rehearse to practice favorable body language and eliminate unfavorable gestures and
     postures. Body language is as important as the verbal message. It should appear natural—not labored or
     uncomfortable. Advisors must recognize that an American stereotype—possibly a negative stereotype—
     likely precedes them. They should try to understand and discern this stereotype.

     A-58. Advisors must develop and employ keen problem-solving skills. Doctrinally accepted U.S. tactics,
     techniques, and procedures must be adapted and articulated in culturally acceptable and supportable terms.
     Advisors must be objective listeners when communicating with their counterparts and remind themselves
     that attacks on ideas are not intended to be personal or deliberately embarrassing. Soldiers must employ
     leadership and instructional techniques appropriate to their role as advisors and to specific regions and
     cultures.

     A-59. Advisors must create balanced training programs to address the deficiencies and needs (rather than the
     preferences) of the HN force. Advisors establish training objectives and maintainable standards, focus on
     combat effectiveness, and praise training accomplishments. Advisors must emphasize the need for
     continuous training and encourage counterparts to maximize training opportunities. Counterparts must
     understand that the training mission needs—

          • Military discipline and leadership.
          • Noncommissioned officer empowerment.
          • Marksmanship and small-unit tactics.
          • Health, strength, and endurance elements.
          • Proper maintenance.
          • Rehearsals.

76  TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                  Advisor Screening and Selection

A-60. Advisors provide training to military, police, and other civilian forces. They must coordinate with other
advisory personnel in the AO and with cooperating U.S. agencies covering psychological operations
(PSYOP) influence activities, agricultural improvements, medical services, population and resources control,
and similar activities.

A-61. Advisors should train their counterparts to use the training resources that they control, to request
additional resources from superiors, and to properly allocate resources to subordinate forces. Advisors should
stress the importance of resources such as ammunition, films, and training aids and show how to construct
and use field-expedient training devices and facilities.

A-62. Advisors must be competent in both formal and informal methods of instruction. When dealing with
an illiterate or semiliterate audience, the advisor should:

     • Determine how people receive their information.
     • Identify the key communicators for the target audience.
     • Identify the informal communications networks (the grapevine) in the community.
     • Include the use of printed materials.
     • Cater visual communication materials to the target audience.

A-63. Advisors must use the U.S. chain of command to obtain assistance and disseminate guidance. Care
must be taken to distinguish between the U.S. chain of command and the HN chain of command. Advisors
provide recommendations—not orders—to their counterparts. Only the counterpart should issue orders to
subordinates.

A-64. Advisors must keep their counterparts informed of advice given to their subordinates. Fellow U.S.
personnel should be kept informed of advice offered to counterparts. HN officials should be persuaded to
pass information up, down, and across the chain of command. If warranted by the HN military customs,
senior-ranking personnel should be saluted, referred to by their rank (as customs permit), and shown respect.
Effectiveness is greatly enhanced when the advisor displays respect for the counterpart and the HN chain of
command.

A-65. Finally, all U.S. advisor rotations must end at some point, and often, a U.S. replacement follows. It is
imperative to have enough time allocated during the relief in place and transfer of authority beforehand to
sensitize the new advisor to the environment and counterparts.

                                                Appendix B

                    Advisor Team Leader Checklist

    This checklist serves as a comprehensive guide to ensure that the Advisor Team is well-
    organized, fully equipped, and ready to address the unique challenges and responsibilities that
    result from deploying as a Team.

     B-1. The recommended tasks required to accomplish advisor missions are:
          • Deploy the team.
          • Provide command and control (C2).
          • Advise foreign security forces (FSF).
          • Protect
          • Sustain
          • Redeploy the team.

TASK 1: DEPLOY THE TEAM

     B-2. Deploying as a team involves the coordinated movement and establishment of your advisor team and
     its resources into a new operational environment. This task requires careful planning, preparation, and
     execution to ensure a smooth and safe transition.

BUILD AN EFFECTIVE TEAM

     B-3. Building an effective team starts with fostering open communication, mutual trust, and a shared
     commitment to common goals.

          • Select team members based on subject matter expert (SME) skills, advisor knowledge, cross-cultural
                communications skills, teaching ability, regional orientation, and mission objectives.

          • Internally task organize by ability, function, and personality.
          • Conduct team building activities, tasks, or functions.
          • Conduct advisor training.

CONDUCT PREDEPLOYMENT PREPARATION

     B-4. The Team must conduct a comprehensive pre-deployment preparation by completing individual and
     collective tasks, analyzing relevant reports, conducting site surveys and area studies, and ensuring Leahy
     Amendment vetting.

          • Conduct individual, collective, unit, and directed tasks. Understand and employ planning
                considerations for the advisor environment.

          • Identify and analyze any existing reports, assessments, or after-action reviews (AARs) from similar
                engagements.

          • Conduct a predeployment site survey (PDSS). Refer to appendix B for an example of a PDSS
                checklist.

          • Conduct an area study and intelligence preparation of the operational environment.
          • Ensure Leahy Amendment vetting of foreign security forces (FSF) has been completed.

TASK 2: COMMAND AND CONTROL

CONDUCT PLANNING FOR ADVISOR-SPECIFIC MISSIONS

     B-5. The Team must conduct Advisor-Specific missions to effectively address the unique requirements and
     objectives of their assignment, ensuring they provide tailored advice, training, and support that meet the
     specific needs of their counterparts and contribute to the overall success of the mission

          • Conduct concurrent team planning for all FSF events.
          • Conduct planning for movement to each mission location.
          • Understand the rank, gender, and age perceptions of the advisor team while working with the FSF

                and local populace.

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  79


---

Appendix B

          • Conduct planning for engagements with critical stakeholders.

COORDINATE AND DECONFLICT WITH HIGHER, SUPPORTING, AND ADJACENT UNITS
(COALITION AND FSF)

     B-6. The Team must coordinate with all other organizations within the AO.
          • Identify higher, supporting, and adjacent units.
          • Establish rapport and communication with higher, supporting, and adjacent units.
          • Advise and influence higher, supporting, and adjacent units on the advisor mission and
                requirements.
          • Execute reporting procedures and requirements.
          • Conduct an assessment across the FSF capabilities and identify capacity gaps or institutional
                shortfalls for future resources and higher unit regional understanding.

IDENTIFY AND ESTABLISH RAPPORT WITH KEY PERSONNEL IN THE AREA OF OPERATIONS
(AO)

     B-7. The Team needs to identify and establish rapport with Key Personnel in the Area of Operations to build
     trust, facilitate open communication, and gain a deeper understanding of the local context, enabling more
     effective collaboration and successful mission outcomes

          • Identify key FSF personnel.
          • Establish rapport with key FSF personnel.
          • Identify key local civilian, religious, and governance leaders.
          • Establish rapport with key local civilian, religious, and governance leaders.

TASK 3: ADVISE FSF

B-8. An Advisor Team needs to advise the Foreign Security Forces to enhance their capabilities, promote
professionalism, and foster interoperability, ultimately supporting the host nation's ability to maintain stability,
security, and sovereignty. This collaboration helps to build strong partnerships and contributes to regional and
global security efforts.

EFFECTIVELY COMMUNICATE IN AN ADVISING ENVIRONMENT.

B-9. The ability for an advisor to communicate with a counterpart is critical to the success of the advisor mission.
Interpreters are often relied upon to bridge communication gaps, but they are not always available or dependable
for providing an accurate representation of a translated conversation based on language skill or experience
working with the military. Thus, language training for the advisor team is an important aspect of the train-up to
facilitate mission success, and include the following skills:

          • Effectively communicate without an interpreter by—
                 Exchanging common greetings in the language used in the AO.
                 Identifying and employing nonverbal communications common in the AO.
                 Understanding verbal and nonverbal threat indicators common in the AO.

          • Effectively communicate through an interpreter by—
                 Understanding the language and security capabilities of the interpreter.
                 Evaluating the trustworthiness of the interpreter.
                 Understanding the background, cultural, and gender biases of the interpreter.
                 Planning and preparing an interpreter for a communications event.
                 Conducting a meeting or event through the interpreter.
                 Preparing to work with a non-vetted or unfamiliar interpreter.
                 Understanding the regional perceptions of the interpreter based on background, culture, or
                     gender biases to manage employment of the interpreter.
                 Planning a negotiation with the interpreter and mediate a conflict with FSF or key local civilian,
                     religious, and governance leaders.

                                                                                                          Advisor Team Leader Checklist

GAIN AND USE INFLUENCE TO PROGRESS ADVISOR MISSION OBJECTIVES.

B-10. An advisor must be able to influence the counterpart to act in ways that may be different from past
experience and cultural norms. Without the ability to influence the counterpart’s actions, the advisor mission may
fail. Affinity between the advisor and counterpart must exist for true influence to occur. A successful advisor will
put an influence strategy in motion resulting in a voluntary, long-lasting change in the counterpart’s actions
despite the absence of formal authority. Successful influence is gained through a combination of rapport building
techniques used to demonstrate value, credibility, and trust. This can only be achieved when everyone perceives
the other as competent, mature, responsible, and compatible (working toward a common goal). If the advisor can
convey a committed attitude to the FSF counterpart, a long lasting, effective advisor relationship will exist. This
commitment is conveyed by—

          • Demonstrating the professional ability of one’s own military occupation and function on the advisor
                team.

          • Demonstrating to the FSF counterpart that the advisor provides value.
                 Demonstrating SME advisor knowledge.
                 Demonstrating access to the advisor team and coalition support or effects.

          • Determining and employing proper influence techniques.
          • Understanding and employing effective negotiations.

                 Prepare to conduct negotiations with the FSF counterpart.
                 Prepare the FSF counterpart to conduct negotiations with local leaders.
                 Prepare the FSF counterpart to conduct negotiations with coalition forces.
          • Effectively advise higher, supporting, and adjacent units or coalition forces.
                 Build rapport with higher, supporting, and adjacent units or coalition forces.
                 Educate higher, supporting, and adjacent units or coalition forces on the capabilities and

                     limitations of the FSF counterpart and unit.
                 Influence higher, supporting, and adjacent units or coalition forces to progress advisor mission

                     objectives.

ADVISE FSF UNIT MISSION-ESSENTIAL TASK LIST, MISSION OBJECTIVE, OR ASSIGNED

TASKS.

B.11 Advisors must teach, coach, and advise FSF to develop their professional skills. Teaching means to provide
instruction and education to FSF to develop skills and knowledge necessary to do a particular task or mission.
Coaching means to assist a counterpart in reaching the next level of knowledge or skill by practicing those skills
and building on previous teaching. The goal is for the FSF to assume more responsibility for success while the
advisor gives assistance as required. Advising means to provide counterparts with counsel to assist them in
making a decision based on applying knowledge. The distinctive feature is the recipient is responsible for making
the decision while the advisor only provides counsel. Need a lead-in sentence here to introduce the bullet points:

          • Develop an advisor campaign plan.
                 Incorporate FSF objectives into the campaign plan.

          • Assess the FSF unit’s, team’s, or individual’s strengths and capabilities.
                 Develop measures of effectiveness and measures of performance.

          • Employ SME and military occupational specialty and experience bases to support mission.
                 Teach individual and collective tasks.
                 Coach/walk through mission rehearsal and assist.
                 Advise FSF leaders in the mission.
                 Advise the FSF leaders on the benefit of organizational assessments through AARs within the
                     context of the FSF cultural norms.

          • Evaluate effectiveness of the advisor effort.
                 Determine FSF leader development needs and education.
                 Conduct an assessment of the FSF organization to determine capabilities, capacity, or
                     institutional gaps.
