                                                Appendix H

                     Women, Peace, and Security

WOMEN, PEACE, AND SECURITY FRAMEWORK

     H-1. Security cooperation (SC) is a primary mechanism through which the DOD implements the Women,
     Peace, and Security (WPS) Act of 2017 and the 2023 WPS Strategy due to its direct involvement with foreign
     security forces. Through WPS integration into SC, the DOD works to support the ability of Allies and partners
     to build defense capability and institutional capacity that is diverse, inclusive, upholds principles of human
     rights, and capable of responding to the unique security needs of host nation populations. SC with Allies and
     partners is a core component of the Department’s ability to fulfill the requirements of the 2017 WPS Act, the
     2020 WPS Strategic Framework Implementation Plan (SFIP), and 2023 WPS Strategy. (2022 U.S.
     Government WPS Congressional Report, July 2022).

     H-2. As appropriate, the WPS initiatives should be integrated into SC planning and considered for SC
     activities with Allies and partners. The WPS initiatives are not all-inclusive, and in most cases, planners
     should consult with gender advisors or WPS points of contact within each Combatant Command (CCMD) to
     support planning, execution, and assessment of SC activities. Gender advisors are technical subject-matter-
     experts (SMEs) responsible for integrating a gender perspective in military operations, in accordance with
     WPS guidance (they may sometimes be referred to as WPS advisors or as WPS program managers, dependent
     upon their command or component-level roles and responsibilities). (JP 3-20).

APPLICATION OF A GENDER PERSPECTIVE

     H-3. The WPS program brings useful women, youth, familial and gender views, and issues impacting the
     populace, as well as course of action (CoA) options. It fits well within the PMESII lens within and is an ideal
     multiplier to security force assistance. (SFA Quarterly, December 2020).

     H-4. DOD’s implementation of WPS initiatives apply a gender perspective into the planning, execution,
     and assessment of SC activities. A crucial component to protecting the human rights of women and children
     is first understanding how perspectives and security needs differ among men, women, boys, and girls within
     the operational environment. By applying a gender perspective, PNs can understand how an action impacts
     men, women, boys, and girls differently based on their gender and sociocultural roles. A gender perspective
     exposes gender differences and how being treated as a man or woman in society shapes a person’s needs,
     interests, and security. A gender perspective sheds light on the power relationships among men, women, and
     children; exposes who has access to and control of resources; and highlights who participates in decision
     making in society. The U.S. can strengthen the capability and capacity of Allies and partners to respond to
     the different needs of those in their society by providing capacity building activities, training, and education
     to defense and security institutions on how to account for and respond to the variation in security needs across
     the population. (JP 3-20).

PLANNING CONSIDERATIONS

     H-5. To plan effectively, it is important to coordinate with interagency partners, civil society, and key
     stakeholders to ensure training and education materials on human rights and international law are tailored to
     the PN context. WPS initiatives should be integrated within DOD missions, authorities, and resources.
     Importantly, DOD WPS efforts should complement DoS and USAID efforts rather than replace them. SC
     Planners should follow the following steps for integrating WPS initiatives within SC activities:

          • Review relevant guidance to identify country-level objectives for Allies and partners. This may
                include combatant campaign plans (CCPs), integrated country strategies (ICSs), country SC plans,
                and component command CSPs/country plans.

          • Conduct a baseline PN gender analysis. A gender analysis is an examination of the relationships and
                interactions between men and women, their access to and control of resources, the constraints they
                face relative to each other, and sociocultural power structures. This should be part of the country-
                level baseline assessment with WPS relevant questions included. The following factors should be
                incorporated into the baseline gender analysis:

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  115


---

Appendix H

                 Institutional, generating force, and operational levels.
                 WPS-related policies.
                 Participation of women in PN military.
                 Sexual violence and gender-based violence.
                 Environmental context (i.e., gender factors in society).
          • Identify WPS supporting initiatives based on PN needs identified in the baseline gender analysis.
                Examples of WPS supporting initiatives include:
                 Increasing number and rank of women in the PN military.
                 Women NCO Corps development.
                 Combatting sexual harassment and assault among own forces.
                 Combatting gender-based violence during operations.
                 Gender analysis of operations.
          • Review significant security cooperation initiatives (SSCIs) and other SC plans and programs to
                determine approach for WPS integration. Examples of significant security cooperation initiatives
                include:
                 WPS can be integrated into existing operations, actions, and activities.
                 WPS activities can be separate, discrete activities within SSCIs.
                 Leverage out-year planning to insert WPS initiatives if not planned for current fiscal year (FY)

                     execution.
                 Just like any other capability, identify end states and objectives and work backwards.
          • Highlight WPS initiatives in relevant proposals submitted to higher headquarters.
          • Include WPS supporting metrics in SC AM&E that:
                 At a minimum, sex-disaggregated participation data should be included (i.e., number of male

                     and female participants from PN military participating in SC activity).
                 Report activities to appropriate gender advisor for the annual data call to meet congressional

                     reporting requirements as stipulated in legislation.
          • The following are planning considerations for integrating WPS principles within SC planning,

                execution, and investments:
                 WPS principles should support the Chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff (CJCS) and CCPs’

                     objectives.
                 WPS activities should build on previous activities each FY and not be one-off events.

                     Maintaining consistency with Allies and partners on one or two WPS principles throughout SC
                     activities generally has the best results.
                 PNs should be prioritized based on presence of WPS national action plans, percent of women
                     in the PN military, level of senior leader support within the PN, and U.S. ambassador support.
                 Do not put WPS where it should not be. WPS should be focused on where it provides the most
                     value for the overarching SC objectives.
                 Allies and partners may be interested, willing, and able to implement WPS principles. PN
                     militaries may already be implementing WPS principles, but they do not use the same
                     terminology or recognize the activity as supporting the global WPS mandate. DOD can build
                     on these existing opportunities.
                 Work with a country SME to better understand the sociocultural norms, behaviors, and
                     interactions between PN men and women. (JP 3-20).

Appendix H

WOMEN, PEACE, AND SECURITY FOCUS AREAS FOR
CAPABILITY & CAPACITY BUILDING ACTIVITIES

     H-8. The following are focus areas for capability and capacity building activities that are essential for
     enhance Allies and Partners’ militaries to effectively recruit, employ, develop, retain and promote individuals
     from diverse backgrounds, addressing organizational weaknesses within defense institutions.

Diversity, equity, and inclusion (DEI)
          Capability and capacity building activities that enable Allies’ and partners’ militaries to better recruit,
          employ, develop, retain, and promote women, people of different races, ethnicities, and other
          intersecting identity characteristics. DEI addresses an organizational weakness within defense
          institutions.

Recruitment and retention
          Capability and capacity building efforts can advise and train ministries on addressing shortfalls in PN
          recruitment and retention of men and women within foreign security forces (FSFs). This will help
          build security organizations that can sustain the workforce needed to employ operational capabilities
          and often suitable to include within human resources programs.

Gender balance/integration
          Establish a roadmap for addressing shortfalls associated with integrating women into the armed forces,
          often suitable to include within human resources programs.

Inclusive leadership development
          Work with Allies’ and partners’ senior leadership at the ministerial level to build core management
          competencies for leading diverse and inclusive defense institutions comprised of men and women. This
          is necessary to create effective, transparent, and accountable defense institutions by establishing an
          environment that prevents stereotyping and discrimination and enables the inclusion and advancement
          of different people in organizations with security missions.

Professionalization of FSFs
          Activities should address organizational weaknesses associated with gender-based violence, including
          sexual harassment and assault, sexual exploitation and abuse, and conflict-related sexual-and gender-
          based violence. Addressing these areas builds effective, transparent, and accountable defense
          institutions.

Sexual harassment and assault
          Addresses the institutions, management competencies, and accountability mechanisms necessary for
          preventing, responding, and holding members of FSF accountable for instances of sexual harassment
          and assault between members.

Sexual exploitation and abuse
          Address the institutions, management competencies, and accountability mechanisms necessary for
          preventing and responding to allegations of sexual exploitation and abuse committed by a member of
          the FSF against a host nation civilian.

Conflict-related sexual and gender violence
          FSF may often encounter survivors of conflict related sexual and gender violence during post conflict
          or stabilization activities. Activities may be used to train ministry level officials on roles and
          responsibilities of security organizations for addressing conflict related sexual and gender-based
          violence, and to develop the internal controls, management competencies, and accountability
          mechanisms to ensure FSF are trained and equipped to prevent and respond to instances of conflict
          related sexual violence.

120  TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                              Women, Peace, and Security

Protection of civilians
          FSF are often called upon to protect civilians during post conflict and stabilization activities by
          preventing and responding to threats of physical violence against civilians, with consideration for the
          different protection and security needs of women and children. Activities may be used to train ministry
          level officials on roles and responsibilities of security organizations for protecting civilians and
          children affected by armed conflict and developing internal controls and management competencies to
          provide oversight of FSF, and/or establishing accountability mechanisms.

Protection of children affected by armed conflict
          FSF are often called upon to protect children affected by armed conflict specifically. This includes
          institutions, oversight, and management mechanisms that enable FSF to detect and respond to
          recruitment and use of children; the killing and maiming of children; sexual violence against children;
          attacks on schools and hospitals; abduction of children; and denial of humanitarian access.

Countering trafficking in persons
          Activities that establish responsible governance and internal controls for enabling security
          organizations to recognize, address, and prevent trafficking in persons. This is a particularly useful
          WPS focus area for capability and capacity building activities in support of counterterrorism, counter-
          illicit drug trafficking, counter-transnational organized crime, and maritime and border security
          operations.

Building the gender advisory workforce of PNs’ gender advisors and gender focal points
          Gender advisors are technical subject matter experts responsible for integrating a gender perspective in
          military operations. DOD can train PN gender advisors and gender focal points assigned to the foreign
          ministry of defense or those assigned to regional organizations with security missions as part of
          capability and capacity building activities.

Gender analysis within defense institutions
          A gender analysis is an examination of the relationships and interactions between men and women, the
          constraints they face relative to each other, and sociocultural power structures. Capability and capacity
          building activities can provide ministerial level advise or training on integrating gender analysis as part
          of building core competencies and capabilities to manage defense related processes.

                                                 Appendix I

                            Embassy Formation

EMBASSY INFORMATION

     I-1. Embassies are the focal point for U.S. government representatives in foreign countries. The embassy
     is normally in the capital; however, there may also be consulate generals, sometimes referred to as consulates,
     established in other cities depending on the size of the country and the need to extend its services across a
     larger area. Collectively, the U.S. presence in a foreign country is referred to as a mission.

     I-2. The chancery is the main building on the embassy ground that houses the main offices of the country
     team. Large embassies often have annexes within the compound. The staff normally live in apartments or
     houses within the local community but may stay within the embassy grounds, if necessary, because of
     security concerns. The embassy and consulates are considered part of U.S. territory; however, the host
     country is responsible for protecting the grounds.

     I-3. Collectively, embassy staff consists of representatives of the government agencies and organizations
     resident within the mission, as well as the various sections and offices that align with the various Department
     of State (DoS) bureaus and career tracks. Embassy and consulate staffing is made up of the interagency group
     consisting of foreign service officers (FSOs) and locally employed staff (LES) that carry out the daily
     responsibilities of the embassy or consulate.

     I-4. The physical size of the embassy, staffing, and organization varies based on location and the diplomatic
     requirements particular for each host nation. There is an adage that states, “If you’ve seen one embassy,
     you’ve seen one embassy.” Figure I-1 shows the organization of a generic country team with the positions
     normally represented in some form. Small embassies or consulates may only have one FSO and an LES
     representing a section where larger embassies or consulates may have much larger numbers.

                                Figure I-1. Generic country team structure

I-5. The ambassador is the head of the country team which consists of the deputy chief of mission, section
heads of all the departments or U.S. government organizations within the embassy, and other personnel as
the ambassador desires. The country team meets on a regular basis and is responsible for conducting

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  123


---

Appendix I

     interagency coordination in the planning and implementation of foreign policy activities and operations
     within the country. Members outside of the DoS are responsible for informing and coordinating their actions
     with their parent organization. Local issues that have regional or worldwide implications may rise to the
     national level for resolution.

EMBASSY STRUCTURE

     I-6. An embassy is the official diplomatic representation of one country in another, and its structure is
     designed to facilitate various diplomatic functions efficiently. At the top of the hierarchy is the Ambassador,
     who serves as the chief of mission and the primary representative of their home country. Supporting the
     Ambassador are several key sections, including the political section, which handles political relations and
     negotiations; the economic section, which focuses on trade and economic affairs; the consular section, which
     provides services to citizens of the home country; and the public diplomacy section, which manages cultural
     and educational exchanges. Additionally, there are administrative and support sections that handle logistics,
     security, and other essential operations. Each of these sections works together to promote the interests of the
     home country and foster strong bilateral relations."

AMBASSADOR

     I-7. The ambassador, also known as the chief of mission (CoM), is the personal representative of the
     President and the highest-ranking U.S. diplomat in the country. Ambassadors are responsible for all U.S.
     policy and interagency actions within the country. The ambassador is either a career FSO or political
     appointee of the President. The number of careers versus political appointees vary by administration but
     between 60 to 70 percent are career foreign service. Ambassadors are the most visible representative to the
     host nation and the chief spokesperson for the embassy.

     I-8. Section 207 of the Foreign Service Act of 1980 codifies the authority of the CoM and provides
     considerable power and authority over what happens within the embassy. The CoM is authorized to see all
     communications, except for some by the CIA, conducted by the departments and sections within the embassy.
     Ambassadors can expel or deny entrance of U.S. government employees from the country. Executive branch
     agency employees, with the exception for personnel working under the command of a military area
     commander, working in the country must keep the CoM informed of their activities and comply with all
     directives. Additional information on military members and organizations under CoM authority can be found
     on the Foreign Assistance Manual (FAM).

DEPUTY CHIEF OF MISSION

     I-9. The Deputy Chief of Mission (DCM) is like an executive officer or deputy commander within the
     military. DCMs are second in charge and responsible for the management of embassy operations and ensures
     the country team works together in support of the mission. The DCM plays a key role in planning major
     events, such as presidential and integrated country strategy (ICS) visits. They also look after work-life
     balance and quality of life issues within the embassy and broader staff.

CHARGÉ D’ AFFAIRES

     I-10. The Chargé d’ Affaires, or often referred to as the “Chargé” is the person, usually the DCM, in charge
     of the mission in the absence of an ambassador. This person has the same rights, privileges, and authority as
     an ambassador, but not the formal rank.

CONSULS GENERAL

     I-11. Consuls Generals (sometimes known as CGs) are subordinate to the ambassador and in charge of
     principal officers of U.S. Consulate Generals that may be in the country. In some cases, such as in China,
     there are several Consulate Generals located in the country, with each headed by a Consul General. In the
     context of an embassy, Consuls General are the most senior consular officers and manage their own sections
     at the embassy as well as providing guidance and oversight of the sections of any Consulate Generals present
     in country.

124  TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                                          Embassy Formation

REGIONAL SECURITY OFFICER

     I-12. Regional Security Officers (RSOs) are sworn federal law enforcement officers responsible for the
     safety and security of all diplomatic personnel, facilities, and classified information. They are the focal point
     for all force protection matters and publish emergency action plans (EAPs) that cover actions in natural
     disasters, civil unrest, and other emergencies. The RSO supervises the security guard detachment of U.S.
     Marines if one is present at the mission. The RSO provides an introductory security orientation briefing to
     all newly assigned embassy personnel.

OFFICE MANAGEMENT SPECIALIST

     I-13. Office management specialists perform a wide range of administrative, operational, and technical
     program support to the ambassador and DCM. Their functions may include running the embassy front office
     and providing general support of oversight of office budgets, planning, and scheduling meetings, and
     preparing written materials.

MANAGEMENT SECTION

     I-14. The management section is responsible for daily embassy operations that include logistics,
     administrative, human resources, financial matters, the motor pool, and facilities.

POLITICAL SECTION

     I-15. The political section reports on the host country’s internal political developments and external relations
     and seeks to advance bilateral and multilateral U.S. policy objectives. Like their economic officer colleagues,
     they work to build relationships within government, business, nongovernmental organizations, and academia.
     Their reporting analysis informs U.S. policy formulation.

PUBLIC DIPLOMACY

     I-16. The public diplomacy section engages with media and local organizations to promote U.S. interests.
     They also manage culture and information programs to increase foreign understanding of U.S. society.

CONSULAR SECTION

     I-17. The Consul General heads a consular section and assists U.S. citizens in the host nation and facilitates
     the adjudication of visas, passports, reports of birth, other documents, and notarial services. Consular officers
     provide emergency services to citizens by visiting them in jail and hospitals, assisting U.S. citizen families
     arranging repatriation of remains and effects following an American death overseas, and assisting with
     evacuation in conflict or natural disaster.

ECONOMIC SECTION

     I-18. The economic section promotes U.S. economic and commercial interests by building relationships with
     their counterparts in the business, government, and academia realms. Their reporting and analysis inform
     U.S. economic and trade policy.

SENIOR DEFENSE OFFICIAL/DEFENSE ATTACHÉ

     I-19. The Senior Defense Official/Defense Attaché (SDO/DATT) is the CoM’s principal diplomatically
     accredited military advisor and representative of all Department of Defense (DOD) elements within the
     country team, except for the Marine Security Guard detachment. The SDO/DATT is the single point of
     contact for all DOD elements working within or from the embassy. Department of Defense Directive
     (DODD) 5205.75 covers the duties and responsibilities of the SDO/DATT.

DEFENSE ATTACHÉ OFFICE

     I-20. The Defense Attaché Office (DAO) consists of service attachés as representatives of the military
     services in the host country. The Defense Intelligence Agency manages the Defense Attaché System, which
     oversees DAO staffing at embassies. The DAO will manage security assistance programs if there is not a
     security cooperation organization (SCO) within the embassy organization.

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  125


---

Appendix I

SECURITY COOPERATION ORGANIZATION

     I-21. The SCO is the DO

     I-22. D entity in the embassy responsible for overseeing security cooperation and security assistance (SA)
     with the host nation. SCO functions and responsibilities may vary at each embassy.

     I-23. SCO is a generic term. The actual name varies by embassy although the duties and responsibilities are
     basically the same. Some of the more common names are U.S. Military Advisory Group, Office of Defense
     Cooperation, and Office of Military Cooperation. The Foreign Affairs Handbook, 2 FAM- 2 H-113.2 CoM
     Authority and the Department of Defense, subparagraph b, provides a partial list of SCO names. Check the
     embassy website or contact the SCO directly to confirm the correct name.

     I-24. The four main sources of authorities come from U.S. laws, DOD policy and instructions, Defense
     Security Cooperation Agency guidance, and from the supporting geographic combatant command (GCC).
     The SCO manages both SA and SC activities which fall under different legal authorities. SA programs fall
     within the DoS under Title 22 of the U.S. Code and authorized by the Foreign Assistance Act of 1961, the
     Arms Export Control Act of 1976, or other appropriation acts. Additionally, SA provides defense equipment,
     education and training, and other defense-related services. DoS owns and manages these programs but are
     executed by the DOD due to their military nature.

     I-25. SC falls under Title 10 of the U.S. Code and the National Defense Authorization Act. These programs
     provide defense articles, training exercises, information exchanges, and defense relationship building events.

     I-26. The SDO/DATT directly supervises the SCO, which works under the authority of the CoM. The GCC
     maintains administrative control over SCO personnel for evaluations, financial and personnel records. The
     SCO is responsible to the GCC for providing input into the theater campaign plans and the country-specific
     annex. The SDO/DATT resolves any conflicts in guidance or priorities between the CoM and GCC to balance
     the requirements and available resources.

     I-27. SCO staffing varies by embassy based on the SA and SC workload. Defense Security Cooperation
     Agency (DSCA) and the GCC utilize the Joint Manpower Program to determine and validate staffing
     requirements. DOD monitors the number and type of personnel (military, civilian, contractor, and LES) in a
     SCO through an annual reporting requirement. The GCC submits manpower changes approved by the CoM
     through DSCA to the Joint Chiefs of Staff for final approval. Department of Defense Instruction (DODI)
     5132.13 covers SCO staffing.

     I-28. SCO offices may be located within the embassy, a separate building nearby, or within the host nation
     ministry of defense. The type, funding, and number of security cooperation programs in the host country
     determines the size of the SCO. Personnel staffing may include a mix of military, contractor, and locally
     employed staff.

     I-29. The National Guard State Partnership Program (SPP) is a security cooperation program that pairs U.S.
     states with foreign countries in enduring relationships to conduct engagements and capacity building events
     in support of national security objectives. The state will assign a bilateral affairs officer to the SCO to assist
     with planning and executing training and engagement events conducted by the state. The SPP is executed by
     the National Guard Bureau and coordinated with the GCC and CoM of the partner country. More information
     is at https://www.nationalguard.mil/Leadership/Joint-Staff/J-5/International-Affairs-Division/State-
     Partnership-Program/.

LOCALLY EMPLOYED STAFF

     I-30. Embassies employ foreign nationals and resident U.S. citizens eligible to work in the country to support
     the mission. They provide local knowledge and contacts and may serve many years in their position. Their
     capabilities and years of knowledge may result in them having significant responsibilities in overseeing
     programs and functions. The RSO vets locally employed staff (LES) but they do not possess security
     clearances that allows access to classified information.

     I-31. The types of positions vary. LES roles include assistants in any of the embassy sections, or in office or
     program management, professional positions, security guards, drivers, or various other skilled trades. There
     is no single source of open LES positions. Each embassy website maintains job openings and application

126         TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                                          Embassy Formation

     procedures. Typically, the number of LES at an embassy or consulate far exceeds the number of American
     staff.

OTHER ORGANIZATIONS AT THE U.S. EMBASSY

     I-32. In addition to the main diplomatic sections, a U.S. Embassy often houses representatives from various
     other U.S. government agencies, each contributing to the overall mission. The United States Agency for
     International Development (USAID) manages foreign aid and development programs. The Federal Bureau
     of Investigation (FBI) may have agents stationed to assist with law enforcement and investigative matters.
     The United States Department of Agriculture (USDA) promotes agricultural trade and supports food security
     initiatives. Medical professionals ensure the health and well-being of embassy staff and may assist with
     public health efforts. The Marine Security Guard Detachment provides security and protects classified
     information, ensuring the safety of the embassy and its personnel. These organizations work collaboratively
     under the ambassador's leadership to advance U.S. interests and support the host country."

UNITED STATES AGENCY FOR INTERNATIONAL DEVELOPMENT

     I-33. The amount, type, and complexity of U.S. assistance determines U.S. Agency for International
     Development (USAID) representation in the host country. A mission director is usually the head of the
     USAID contingent of FSOs and local staff. A mission Military-Civil Coordinator (MC2) manages DOD
     engagement and facilitates military cooperation with USAID specialists in non-disaster operations. They are
     the point of contact for any DoD-USAID coordination when present at a mission.

FEDERAL BUREAU OF INVESTIGATION

     I-34. The Federal Bureau of Investigation (FBI) maintains legal attaches (sometimes known as legates) in
     93 offices in U.S. embassies and consulates around the world. The agents work with local and international
     authorities to share information, investigate, and apprehend criminal and terrorist organizations that threaten
     U.S. national security. Agents will also conduct training with the host nation’s law enforcement officials to
     build interoperability and capacity. Additional information is available on the FBI website at
     https://www.fbi.gov/.

UNITED STATES DEPARTMENT OF AGRICULTURE

     I-35. The Foreign Agricultural Service (FAS) within the United States Department of Agriculture (USDA)
     maintains FSOs at embassies to facilitate trade and represent the interests of the U.S. agricultural sector. The
     agricultural attaché promotes and expands access for U.S. products in the host nation. They also provide non-
     emergency food aid and technical assistance to foreign countries. The agricultural attaché is focused on
     promoting U.S. interests and products where the USAID agricultural officers are interested in host nation
     development. Additional information is available on the USDA website at: https://www.fas.usda.gov/.

MEDICAL PROFESSIONALS

     I-36. The Bureau of Medical Services provides regional medical officers (RMOs), nurse practitioners,
     physician assistants, psychiatrists, and laboratory technicians to support embassies and consulates. The
     number and specialties of the medical providers is based on the size of the mission and capabilities of local
     healthcare. Additional information can be found at https://careers.state.gov/career-paths/worldwide-foreign-
     service/specialist/medical-andhealth/.

MARINE SECURITY GUARD DETACHMENT

     I-37. There are 2,450 Marine Security Guards at 150 missions around the world. A staff sergeant (E-6) or
     above leads the Marine Security Guard Detachment (MSGD), which works with the RSO to protect sensitive
     information and facilities. The MSGD also assists the RSO with controlling access to embassy grounds and
     responding to bomb threats; demonstrations; and nuclear, biological, and chemical attacks.

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  127


---

Appendix I

LOCAL LAW, DIPLOMATIC IMMUNITY, AND STATUS OF FORCES
AGREEMENTS

     I-38. Local Law refers to the legal system and regulations of the host country where an embassy is located,
     which typically applies to both local citizens and foreign residents but may have exceptions for diplomatic
     personnel. Diplomatic Immunity is a principle of international law that exempts diplomats from the
     jurisdiction of the host country's courts, protecting them from prosecution or detention, except in certain
     exceptional circumstances. This immunity is essential for diplomats to perform their duties without fear of
     local interference. Status of Forces Agreements (SOFAs) are agreements between a host country and a
     foreign nation that station military forces within its territory. SOFAs outline the legal status and rights of
     military personnel, addressing issues like criminal jurisdiction, taxation, and the use of facilities. Together,
     these concepts help govern the interactions between host countries and foreign diplomatic or military
     presences, facilitating smooth and respectful relations."

DIPLOMATIC IMMUNITY

     I-39. Diplomatic immunity for specific members of the embassy staff has a basis in international law. The
     two primary international agreements are the Vienna Convention on Diplomatic Relations (VCDR) of 1961
     and the Vienna Convention on Consular Relations (VCCR) of 1969.

     I-40. The embassy staff and close family members are typically subject to privileges and immunities from
     local laws based on their status as accredited members of a diplomatic mission or consular post. Being an
     accredited member requires several factors: the individual lives in the metropolitan area, works at least 90
     consecutive days, are employed full-time (at least 35 hours per week), and their work supports the diplomatic
     mission. There are several limitations to diplomatic immunity. Diplomats must still obey local laws and pay
     speeding and parking tickets that result in a fine but not a court appearance. The ownership of real or personal
     property, other than within an official capacity, is not protected from litigation. LES are citizens and residents
     in the host country and not typically provided diplomatic immunity.

     I-41. The Foreign Affairs Manual at https://fam.state.gov/ and the DoS website at https://www.state.gov/
     provides additional information on diplomatic immunity.

STATUS OF FORCES AGREEMENTS (SOFAS)

     I-42. SOFAs address the status of U.S. armed forces and the application of local laws within a country. Most
     of these are bilateral agreements but some, such as within the North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO)
     countries, are multilateral. SOFAs also may cover taxes and fees, customs regulations, wearing of uniforms,
     carrying of weapons, and driver’s licensing. SOFAs are only applicable during peacetime and become void
     in time of war.

     I-43. A SOFA does not provide the same protection as diplomatic immunity. The agreement may designate
     exclusive or shared jurisdiction with the signatory country over prosecuting criminal and/or civil offenses by
     U.S. personnel. The U.S. and host country often share jurisdiction with each having specific areas of
     responsibility A country may request the other signatory waive jurisdiction based on the circumstances of
     the offense, but it does not have to be honored.16 The Office of Security Negotiations and Agreements in the
     U.S. Department of State Bureau of Political-Military Affairs is responsible for leading the negotiation and
     signing of SOFAs. A list of current SOFAs can be found on the DoS website at. Table I-1 lists the rank
     equivalencies of military, civilian, and foreign service officers.

KEY PEOPLE WITHIN THE EMBASSY STAFF

     I-44. While it is good to be aware of Embassy Staff presence and functions, they do not all normally have
     direct involvement with the military elements at the embassy. However, there are several key members that
     may have a direct impact on military activities in the host nation. In some cases, such as a training team in
     the host country for a short time, the interaction will be through the SDO/DATT or SCO. It is important to
     maintain good relations with the people listed below:

          • Ambassador

29 January 2025                    TC 3-22                                             129


---

Appendix I

                 It is essential to have the full faith, confidence, and trust of the ambassador.
          • Deputy Chief of Mission

                 The DCM may often act as a gatekeeper to the ambassador; therefore, it is a good idea to
                     establish a personal relationship with the DCM to facilitate the free flow of information and
                     counsel.

          • Consul General (CG)
                 It is a good practice to ensure the CG is aware of any military engagements or activities in their
                     area. Offer to provide the CG briefings or after-action reports to ensure they have situational
                     awareness and inform their own reporting requirements.

          • Office Management Specialist for the Ambassador and DCM
                 They have no official power but can facilitate access to the ambassador and DCM.

          • SDO/DATT and SCO
                 A military team that will be in the host nation for an extended period conducting a complex
                     mission should consider placing a liaison officer in the SCO to assist with planning and
                     communication.

          • Regional Security Officer
                 It is important to maintain a good relationship and gain the confidence of the RSO. Take their
                     concerns and advice into account and do not become a force protection liability.

          • Management Officer
                 Establishing a good relationship with the management office staff is helpful in gaining support
                     for in facility, contracting, and logistics operations areas.

          • Public Affairs Officer
                 Compare the embassy guidance with that given by the military public affairs officer to see if
                     there are any contradictions. Ask for approved messages, talking points, and anticipated
                     questions and answers to prepare Soldiers for media engagements.

          • Locally Employed Staff (LES)
                 Their knowledge of the local culture, host nation government, and inner workings of the U.S.
                     embassy can be a great benefit. LES do not have security clearances, so practice good
                     operational security measures when in their presence.

             Source: Military Guide to the U.S. Embassy, Center for Army Lessons Learned, NO. 23-05 (730)
             2023.

                               Appendix K

                               Socium

INTRODUCTION TO THE SOCIUM GUIDEBOOK

K-1. This document establishes the U.S. Army Socium Guidebook for Service Security Cooperation (SC)

stakeholders     involved  in  Socium.  This  Guidebook  supplements                                  the

https://www.milsuite.mil/book/groups/socium (Includes SAAR forms created and maintained by the Defense

Security Cooperation Agency (DSCA). This guidebook, applicable to the Army’s Total Force, is intended to

remove procedural ambiguity, standardize data entry, and maximize data fidelity. This guidebook is not

intended to supersede either DSCA or Combatant Command (CCMD) guidance. However, in the absence of

CCMD guidance regional stakeholders will adhere to the policies and guidance in this Guidebook.

BACKGROUND OF THE SOCIUM GUIDEBOOK

K-2. Socium, Latin for “partner,” replaces the Global Theater Security Cooperation Management
Information System (G-TSCMIS) as the authoritative Security Cooperation Database for the Department of
Defense. Socium will facilitate and integrate planning, budgeting, collaboration, design, management,
assessment, monitoring, evaluation, and reporting for all U.S. Security Cooperation (SC) activities.

K-3. Socium performs each function in a manner that meets the mandate of the FY17 National Defense
Authorization Act (NDAA) and later NDAA reforms by enabling Assessment, Monitoring, and Evaluation
(AM&E) and Defense Institution Building.

K-4. Socium introduces a comprehensive lifecycle management system for (SC) operations, activities, and
investments (OAIs). Additionally, it enables the community to address long-standing issues, such as (1)
building and streamlining the approval process and strategic alignment of Significant Security Cooperation
Initiatives (SSCIs); (2) version control, collaboration, and development of the Training and Equipment Lists
(TELs); (3) converting prose into structured data to enable business analytics; (4) archiving, uploading, and
searching community documentation to aid knowledge transfer and records management; (5) centralizing an
AM&E framework to house pertinent data points to improve execution; and (6) interfacing with other
authoritative data sources to increase knowledge and reduce data-entry.

K-5. HQMC, Plans, Policies, & Operations (PP&O), Strategy & Plans Division (PL), International Affairs
Branch (PLU) is responsible for acting as a liaison between USMC stakeholders and DSCA, identifying gaps
and recommending changes to Socium.

ADMINISTRATION

K-6. Entries into the Socium interface tool are designed to take less than ten minutes. SOCIUM users should
become familiar with operating the SOCIUM interface by reviewing training materials. The role of ‘Socium
Manager’ is designed as a collateral duty and will involve a moderate administrative responsibility.

LINKS AND RESOURCES

K-7. Stakeholders involved in Socium should use the following resources to gain a thorough understanding
of the guidance and policies for Socium.

     • Socium: https://socium.dsca.mil/
     • SIPR Socium: https://socium.dsca.smil.mil/
     • Socium Test Site: https://socium.test.dsca.mil/
     • Socium Milsuite Site: https://www.milsuite.mil/book/groups/socium (Includes SAAR forms)

          Socium GIS Guide:
           https://gis.apps.dsca.mil/portal/apps/storymaps/stories/975f320919cb41dfbfd7fca2bb88acc3
     • Initiatives Reference Guide: https://www.milsuite.mil/book/docs/DOC-1151672

SIPR ACCESS

     K-12. For Socium users with an active NIPR account who need access to Socium SIPR, follow the SAAR
     modification process to request Socium SIPR access:

          • Send a digitally signed email requesting access to Socium SIPR to their supervisor.
          • After supervisors verify users’ need for access, proper access level, and Annual Information

               Awareness (AIA) training requirements, they will send a digitally signed email to their
               organization’s designated OIO requesting access on behalf of the user.
          • OIOs will review, digitally sign, and send their requests to DSCA Service Desk at
               dsca.mechanicsburg.imt.mbx.servicedesk@mail.mil.
          • Users must have an active Secret or higher clearance verified by their security manager and have
               completed the AIA training within one (1) year.

USER TYPES

     K-13. As users fill out the SAAR form, the user will need to request access to specific roles in the system.
     The roles are not mutually exclusive, meaning a user can request multiple.

134                                          TC 3-22                        29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                                                            Socium

ORGANIZATION

     K-14. Users may only be assigned to one organization. Army stakeholders must determine whether their
     work focuses primarily on a Geographic Command or the Service as a whole.

AUTHORITIES

     K-15. Users can select which legal authorities authorize the activity they record in Socium, including Title
     10 Chapter 16 authorities (i.e., 333, 332) and other Title 10 authorities (I.e. Foreign Disaster Assistance).
     Activities are recorded through unique workflows, such as Country Strategies, Strategic Security Cooperation
     Initiatives (SSCIs), and the General Activity Workflow (GAW), which serves as a catch all for all remaining
     activities. All USMC users should request access to the GAW and all other authorities they are likely to plan
     events under during their tour in the billet. Users should verify the legal authorities used to authorize the
     activities recorded in Socium to ensure they are recorded appropriately. Note that users may create an entry
     associated with one organization, and users in a supporting organization may create and link the supporting
     entry to the original one.

REGION

     K-16. Users can align themselves to activities in certain Areas of Responsibility (AORs). Service-wide users
     can select all of the CCMDs.

ROLES

     K-17. Users can request different roles on the SAAR forms when they create a Socium account. Users are
     encouraged to request multiple roles. The various roles are briefly explained in Figure K-1, but Socium
     Training 102 provides a more in-depth analysis of the roles and how they work in Socium.

                                              Figure K-1. User Permissions

Analyst
     K-18. Analysts can view any workflow in Socium but have no editing permissions. This is recommended
     for decision makers and users who may need situational awareness of SC activities but are not directly
     involved in planning or implementation.

29 January 2025  TC 3-22  135


---

Appendix K

Activity Planner (Recommended)
     K-19. Activity Planners are the most common role in Socium and the role closest to most users in the legacy
     system, Global Theater Security Cooperation Management System (G-TSCMIS). Activity Planners create
     activities and oversee planning actions. This role is a useful catch all for those who do not exactly align to
     other roles.

Strategic Planner
     K-20. Any user who assists in the development of country strategies or SSCIs should select the strategic
     planner role. This is recommended for stakeholders with country-specific portfolios such as desk officers.

Contributor (Recommended)
     K-21. Contributors can support and add content to any workflow, including those from other organizations.
     This is recommended for most USMC Socium Users for situational awareness of other activities and to avoid
     dual reporting.

Implementing Agency Manager
     K-22. The Implementing Agency Manager represents an agency. These users can create/edit data in the
     planning phase to review feasibility of activities and create/update data in the execution phase.

Reviewer
     K-23. The Reviewer role is recommended for any user whose portfolio includes planning and review cycles.
     This is highly recommended for users who work on or review SSCIs.

Financial Integration Manager
     K-24. These DSCA business users work to integrate financial data with other systems; this role is not
     applicable to USMC users.

AM&E Manager
     K-25. AM&E Managers work on Assessments, Monitoring, and Evaluation processes, as defined in DODI
     5132.14 and subsequent polices.

Organizational Data Manager
     K-26. These organizational leaders represent the Socium Groups described above and have elevated
     permission levels. Users should contact their OIO if they are interested in being Organizational Data
     Managers (ODMs), as there are a limited number of ODMs per organization.

System Administrator
     K-27. System Administrators must be part of the DSCA development team; this role is not applicable to
     USMC users.

ORGANIZATIONAL ROLES AND RESPONSIBILITIES

     K-28. Users should use Socium throughout the SC planning and execution process. Some databases, such as
     NIPR Command and Control of the Information Environment (C2IE) may push data to Socium. The
     elimination of dual entry should be a priority for all Socium users. All Socium users should ensure their
     entries are clearly labeled and are not duplicative of existing data. Introductory sentence for the bullet list
     needed here:

          • OSD and Joint Staff: OSD and Joint Staff may report OSD and Joint Staff Engagements in Socium.
          • Combatant Command: In conjunction with other tools, CCMDs will have visibility of all SC

                activities in their AOR. CCMDs should enter all CCMD-led SC activities into Socium.

136  TC 3-22  29 January 2025


---

                                                                                                                                            Socium

          • The Services: The Services should report all SC activities not already reported by the CCMDs,
                Component Commands, or other implementing agency. Please refer to the Legacy Record
                Association Tool on the homepage to retrieve legacy entries from G-TSCMIS, Intelink, and
                Combatant Command (CCMD) Theater Information Security System (C-TSCMIS), or search for
                SC activities in the Activity List before creating a new activity. The Services may either be a
                resource provider for an SC event, or they may be the executor of the event. The Services may
                conduct SC in the form of events, activities, or exercises, or by providing training opportunities at
                the Service’s professional military educational institutions. They may also conduct staff talks,
                senior leader engagements, or seminars.

NIPR AND SIPR

     K-29. Socium is operational for NIPR and SIPR and will continue to be developed. Socium SIPR will use
     line-item classification, but all unclassified aspects of an entry will appear on NIPR. The connection between
     NIPR and SIPR is not instantaneous— there is a 24-hour lag between when updates made on the NIPR side
     appear on SIPR and when unclassified components of an entry made on SIPR appear on the NIPR side. Also,
     documents will not transfer across domains. For example, documents uploaded on NIPR will not appear on
     SIPR.

SOCIUM TRAINING

     K-30. Available training courses included in the training package for Socium are provided for those who
     require training. Users may create a Defense Acquisition University account to access Virtual Campus by
     visiting https://www.dau.edu/faq/p/New-DAU-Account. After account creation, users may search “SOC” in
     the search bar and view the Socium courses: https://dau.csod.com/GlobalSearch/search.aspx?s=&q=Socium.
     An overview of each course includes:

          • SOC-101 Introduction to Socium: This web-based training teaches basic end users to perform basic
                navigation within Socium to plan, create, and manage entries for security cooperation activities and
                initiatives. It delivers an opportunity to perform key tasks, including the review and approval of
                activity workflows.

          • SOC-102 Roles and Rules in Managing Security Cooperation Activities: This web-based training
                teaches basic end users to differentiate user roles, responsibilities, and permissions in Socium. It
                includes an overview of user data role interactions within a workflow lifecycle.

          • SOC-103 Workflows, Legal Authorities, and Data Reporting: This web-based training provides an
                overview of workflows and associated legal authorities for managing SC activities. It includes
                detailed guidance on how to use data elements, such as the Training and Equipment List (TEL) to
                support an activity workflow.

          • SOC-104 Strategic Planning and Advanced Data Reporting: This web-based training provides an
                overview of strategic planning and the strategy to capability (S2C) framework. It also includes an
                overview of data outputs related to SSCIs.

          • SOC-104 Socium Strategic Planning – Five Year Plan: The course shows users how to interact with
                the 5-Year Plan tool in the Strategic Planning workflow of Socium.

          • SOC-105: Working in the SIPR Environment in Socium: This course provides an overview of how
                to manage security cooperation initiatives and activities within the SIPR environment in Socium.

SOCIUM ENTRIES

     K-31. Socium users should input entries for SC events, activities, and exercises that occur between their
     organization and ally and partner nations (PNs), or any activity in support of SC engagements. Users should
     verify with their organizations the authority used to authorize the SC activity to ensure accurate reporting.

     K-32. Legal authorities represented in Socium include the following:
          • Title 10 Chapter 16: §311, §312, §321, §331, §332, §341, §342, §343, §345, §347, §348, §349, §350
          • Title 10 Other: §401, §402, §404, §407, §166a, §2557, §2561, Mil (Mil conducted under other
                authority).
          • Temporary Authorities: FY08 122233, FY02 §1234, FY08 §1234, FY15 §1206, FY18 §1207, FY15
                §1236, FY15 §1535(A)(5), FY16 §1250, FY16 §1251, FY16 §1263.

  SECTION II – TERMS

assessment
          A continuous process that measures the overall effectiveness of employing joint force capabilities
          during military operations. (JP 3-0)

crisis
          An emerging incident or situation involving a possible threat to the United States, its citizens, military
          forces, or vital interests that develops rapidly and creates a condition of such diplomatic, economic, or
          military importance that commitment of military forces and resources is contemplated to achieve
          national and/or strategic objectives. (JP 3-0)

liaison
          That contact or intercommunication maintained between elements of military forces or other agencies
          to ensure mutual understanding and unity of purpose and action. (FM 6-0)

foreign assistance
          Support for foreign nations that can be provided through development assistance, humanitarian
          assistance, and security assistance. (JP 3-0)

military engagement
          Contact and interaction between individuals or elements of the Armed Forces of the United States and
          those of another nation’s armed forces, or foreign and domestic civilian authorities or agencies, to
          build trust and confidence, share information, coordinate mutual activities, and maintain influence. (JP
          3-0).

rules of engagement
          Directives issued by competent military authority that delineate the circumstances and limitations
          under which United States forces will initiate and/ or continue combat engagement with other forces
          encountered. (JP 3-84)

security cooperation
          Department of Defense interactions with foreign security establishments to build relationships that
          promote specific United States security interests, develop allied and partner military and security
          capabilities for self-defense and multinational operations, and provide United States forces with
          peacetime and contingency access to allies and partners. (JP 3-20).

security force assistance
          The Department of Defense activities that support the development of the capability and capacity of
          foreign security forces and their supporting institutions. (JP 3-20)

29 January 2025   TC 3-22                                    143


---

Glossary

security forces
          Duly constituted military, paramilitary, police, and constabulary forces of a state. (JP 3-22)

security sector reform
          A comprehensive set of programs and activities undertaken by a host nation to improve the way it
          provides safety, security, and justice. (JP 3-07)

stability operation
          An operation conducted outside the United States in coordination with other instruments of national
          power to establish or maintain a secure environment and provide essential governmental services,
          emergency infrastructure reconstruction, and humanitarian relief. (ADP 3-0)

stabilization activities
          Various military missions, tasks, and activities conducted outside the United States in coordination
          with other instruments of national power to maintain or reestablish a safe and secure environment and
          provide essential governmental services, emergency infrastructure reconstruction, and humanitarian
          relief. (JP 3-0)

support
          The action of a force that aids, protects, complements, or sustains other forces in accordance with a
          directive requiring such action. (JP 1, Volume 2)
